A Tauberian approach to metric scaling limits of random discrete structures, with an application to random planar maps
William FLEURAT

Abstract
. We prove sandwich theorems and a Tauberian theorem in the space of compact metric measure spaces, endowed with the Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov (GHP) topology. These results hold with respect to a close relative of Gromov’s Lipschitz order. As a proof-of-concept of a general method to prove metric scaling limits of random discrete structures, we give an application to the theory of random planar maps: the Brownian sphere is the scaling limit in the GHP topology of irreducible quandrangulations. Our main inputs are (i) the convergence of general quadrangulations to the Brownian sphere (Le Gall, 2013; Miermont, 2013); and (ii) couplings where irreducible quadrangulations of the hexagon are “grown” by face-openings (Addario-Berry, 2014).



1.
Introduction

This work aims to present a novel approach to “descend” metric properties of large random discrete structures from a
host
model to a related
component
model, in a condensation regime.
For concreteness, we let
H_{n}
(resp.
C_{n}
) denote a uniformly random size-
n
object from the
host
model (resp. from the
component
model).
The
condensation
phenomenon—which is ubiquitous in probabilistic combinatorics, see the related surveys
[
30
,
45
]
and references therein—may be described as follows: with high probability as
n\to\infty
, one can find in
H_{n}
an object
\mathfrak{c}(H_{n})
from the
component
model, which is a random-size version of the
(C_{n})_{n\geq 1}
, with asymptotically linear size:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}(H_{n})\overset{(d)}{=}C_{N(n)},\qquad\text{with}\qquad\frac{N(n)}{n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\theta

in probability, for some
\theta>0
.

It is often possible to transfer asymptotics from
H_{n}
to
\mathfrak{c}(H_{n})
. Since the latter has the same distribution as
C_{N(n)}
, one may therefore hope to extract the asymptotic behavior of
C_{n}
by comparison with
C_{N({m})}
, where
m
is chosen so that
N(m)\approx n
, say
m=\lfloor n/\theta\rfloor
.
While transferring asymptotics from the non-randomized sequence
(C_{n})_{n\geq 1}
to the randomized one
(C_{N(\lfloor n/\theta\rfloor)})_{n\geq 1}
is straightforward, the converse direction that we consider here is a problem of a
Tauberian
nature, which requires ruling out bad behavior by means of an extra
Tauberian assumption
.

To that end, we establish a
Tauberian theorem
in the Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov space of (isometry classes of) compact metric measure spaces, where the Tauberian assumption involves a variant of Gromov’s Lipschitz order
[
27
,
25
]
. Along the way, we prove that the corresponding order satisfies a sandwich theorem of independent interest.

As a proof-of-concept, we prove a new scaling limit result in the theory of random planar maps: random
irreducible quadrangulations
properly normalized converge to the Brownian sphere, a result we obtain by reduction to the case of
general quadrangulations
covered by Le Gall
[
33
]
and Miermont
[
41
]
.
The proof decomposes into a few well-identified and largely independent steps, for which we provide arguments that we believe to be fairly robust. We therefore expect it to find applications beyond the specific model studied in this paper.

1.1.
Sandwich and Tauberian theorems in the GHP space

We denote by
\mathbb{M}
the set of
compact metric measure spaces
—namely triples
\mathcal{X}=(X,d,\mu)
where
(X,d)
is a compact metric space and
\mu
is a
finite
measure on its Borel
\sigma
-algebra—and we denote by
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
the quotient of
\mathbb{M}
with respect to the relation of measure-preserving isometry:
(X,d,\mu)
and
(X^{\prime},d^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
are measure-preserving isometric if there is a bijective mapping
\varphi\colon X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
such that
\varphi_{*}\mu=\mu^{\prime}
and
d^{\prime}(\varphi(x),\varphi(y))=d(x,y)
for all
x,y\in X
.
We denote by
[X,d,\mu]
the measure-preserving isometry class of
(X,d,\mu)\in\mathbb{M}
.
The quotient space
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
may be equipped with the
measured Gromov–Hausdorff
topology
[
21
]
, or
Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov
(GHP) topology, which is induced by the GHP metric
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
, see
[
19
,
40
,
2
]
.
Convergence in the GHP topology will be denoted by the symbol
{\scriptstyle\smash{\xrightarrow{\mathrm{GHP}}}}
.

A partial order on the GHP space

We introduce a partial order on the space
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
which is a variant of Gromov’s Lipschitz order
[
27
,
44
]
, see also Grieshammer and Rippl’s unpublished work
[
25
]
for a closely related variant on the Gromov–Prokhorov (GP) space.

Definition 1.1
.

Given
\mathcal{X}=[X,d,\mu]
and
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=[X^{\prime},d^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}]
in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
, we write
\mathcal{X}\unlhd\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
, if there exists a
surjective

mapping
\varphi\colon X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
which is
1-Lipschitz
, that is:

(1.1)

\displaystyle d\bigl(\varphi(x^{\prime}),\varphi(y^{\prime})\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq d^{\prime}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}),\qquad x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in X^{\prime};

and
measure-contracting
, that is:

(1.2)

\displaystyle\mu(A)

\displaystyle\leq\mu^{\prime}\bigl(\varphi^{-1}(A)\bigr),\qquad A\in\mathcal{B},

where
\mathcal{B}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
(X,d)
.

We note that Definition
1.1
does not depend on the choice of representatives modulo measure-preserving isometry, as it should.
Let us give a concrete example.
We denote by
V(G)
the vertex-set of a graph
G
, which we equip with the graph distance
d_{G}
and counting measure
\mu_{G}
.
Given two graphs
G
and
G^{\prime}
, if there exists a surjective graph homomorphism
G^{\prime}\to G
, then we have
[V(G),d_{G},\mu_{G}]\unlhd[V(G^{\prime}),d_{G^{\prime}},\mu_{G^{\prime}}]
, see Lemma
7.1
.

A sandwich theorem

The binary relation
\unlhd
actually defines a partial order on
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
which interacts nicely with the GHP topology, as evidenced by the following sandwich theorem.

Theorem 1.2

(
GHP Sandwich theorem
)
.

The binary relation
\unlhd
is a partial order on
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
, which obeys the following
sandwich theorem
:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\forall n,\quad\mathcal{X}^{-}_{n}\unlhd\mathcal{X}_{n}\unlhd\mathcal{X}^{+}_{n},\\
\mathcal{X}^{-}_{n}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{GHP}}\mathcal{X}_{\infty},\\
\mathcal{X}^{+}_{n}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{GHP}}\mathcal{X}_{\infty},\end{cases}\qquad\implies\qquad\Bigl[\mathcal{X}_{n}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{GHP}}\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\Bigr],

for all sequences
(\mathcal{X}^{-}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
,
(\mathcal{X}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
, and
(\mathcal{X}^{+}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
of elements of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
, and all
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
.

We note that our proof of this sandwich theorem relies on proving two natural properties of the partial order
\unlhd
, namely that it is
closed
and
down-compact
, see Definition
3.10
and Lemma
3.14
. It is to be expected that natural variants of the order
\unlhd
for other “Gromov–Hausdorff-type” topologies also satisfy those properties and thus a sandwich theorem.
In fact, Grieshammer and Rippl’s partial order on the Gromov–Prokhorov space satisfies these properties—see Prop. 3.2 and Prop. 4.1 in
[
25
]
—and thus also a sandwich theorem. Also, our arguments can be readily adapted to the Gromov–Hausdorff topology by just dropping all mentions of the measures.

A second sandwich theorem

We can go further and ask for a probabilistic version of the above sandwich theorem.
In the sequel, a
random compact metric measure space
is a random variable
\mathfrak{X}
on some probability space, taking values in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
endowed with its Borel
\sigma
-algebra. Its distribution is therefore an element of the space
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}})
of Borel probability measures on
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
.
Naturally,
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}})
is endowed with the topology of weak convergence induced by the GHP topology. Such convergence will be represented by the symbol
{\scriptstyle\smash{\xRightarrow{\mathrm{GHP}}}}
.
Stochastic ordering on
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}})
with respect to
\unlhd
will be denoted by
\unlhd_{\mathrm{st}}
, that is
\P(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\unlhd_{\mathrm{st}}\P^{\prime}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})
if and only if the inequality
\P(A)\leq\P^{\prime}(A)
holds for every
\unlhd
-increasing event
A
—where the latter means that
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\in A
whenever
\mathcal{X}\in A
and
\mathcal{X}\unlhd_{\mathrm{st}}\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
.
We extend Theorem
1.2
as follows.

Theorem 1.3

(
Probabilistic GHP Sandwich theorem
)
.

The binary relation
\unlhd_{\mathrm{st}}
is a partial order on
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}})
, which obeys the following
sandwich theorem
:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\forall n,\>\P^{-}_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\unlhd_{\mathrm{st}}\P_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\unlhd_{\mathrm{st}}\P^{+}_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X}),\\
\P^{-}_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\xRightarrow{\mathrm{GHP}}\P_{\infty}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X}),\\
\P^{+}_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\xRightarrow{\mathrm{GHP}}\P_{\infty}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X}),\end{cases}\mkern-25.0mu\implies\quad\Bigl[\P_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\xRightarrow{\mathrm{GHP}}\P_{\infty}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\Bigr],

for all sequences
(\P^{-}_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X}))_{n\geq 1}
,
(\P_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X}))_{n\geq 1}
, and
(\P^{+}_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X}))_{n\geq 1}
of elements of
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}})
, and all
\P_{\infty}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}})
.

We actually prove in Lemma
3.15
that the two sufficient conditions we singled out for a sandwich theorem to hold (the order being
closed
and
down-compact
) automatically lift to the corresponding stochastic order, provided the underlying space is Polish.

A Tauberian theorem

The prototypical use case we have in mind for Theorem
1.3
is “de-randomizing” or “conditioning” scaling limit results.
Suppose we are trying to prove that
\mathfrak{X}_{n}
properly rescaled converges to some
\mathfrak{X}_{\infty}
in distribution with respect to the GHP topology, but that we only have access to such a convergence for the sequence
(\mathfrak{X}_{N_{n}},n\geq 1)
where
N_{n}\approx n
is a “randomized time” independent from the original sequence
(\mathfrak{X}_{n},n\geq 1)
.
This randomization has an averaging effect on the distribution of the random variables at play, which may well hide oscillatory behavior of the original sequence.

This is strongly reminiscent of
Tauberian problems
in real or complex analysis: given a transform that regularizes a sequence,
Tauberian theorems
provide conditions under which the asymptotic behavior of the transform implies related asymptotics for the original sequence. These
Tauberian
conditions ensure that the original sequence does not behave too wildly.
In our setting, the Tauberian assumption will be phrased in terms of stochastic increase with respect to the order
\unlhd
.

For
a,b>0
and
[X,d,\mu]\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
, we define the rescaled space
(a,b)\cdot[X,d,\mu]=[X,a\cdot d,b\cdot\mu]
.
We say that a positive sequence
(x_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is
tame
if
x_{n+k_{n}}/x_{n}\to 1
whenever
k_{n}=o(n)
.
For instance,
(n^{\alpha}(\log n)^{\beta})_{n\geq 2}
is tame for every
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

Theorem 1.4

(
GHP Tauberian theorem
)
.

Let
\left(\mathfrak{X}_{n},n\geq 1\right)
and
\mathfrak{X}_{\infty}
be random compact metric measure spaces.
Let
(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}
,
(b_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be tame sequences, and let
(N_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be random variables which are independent of
(\mathfrak{X}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and
\mathfrak{X}_{\infty}
.
Assume that:

(1)

[Convergence with randomized time.]

In distribution for the GHP topology,

\displaystyle(a_{n},b_{n})\cdot\mathfrak{X}_{N_{n}}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\mathfrak{X}_{\infty}.

(2)

[Concentration of the randomized time.]

In probability,

\displaystyle\frac{N_{n}}{n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}1.

(3)

[Tauberian assumption.]

The sequence
(\mathfrak{X}_{n},n\geq 1)
is
\unlhd
-stochastically non-decreasing, that is, with
\P_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})
the law of
\mathfrak{X}_{n}
for
n\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\P_{1}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\unlhd_{\mathrm{st}}\P_{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\unlhd_{\mathrm{st}}\P_{3}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\unlhd_{\mathrm{st}}\dots.

Then,
(a_{n},b_{n})\cdot\mathfrak{X}_{n}\to\mathfrak{X}_{\infty}
in distribution for the GHP topology.

Remark 1.5
.

If Assumption
(2)
is replaced by “
N_{n}/n\rightarrow c
in probability”,
c>0
, then we obtain instead that
(a_{\lfloor n/c\rfloor},b_{\lfloor n/c\rfloor})\cdot\mathfrak{X}_{n}\to\mathfrak{X}_{\infty}
in distribution for the GHP topology. This is an easy consequence of the case
c=1
.

1.2.
Application to random planar maps

A (rooted)
planar map
is a proper embedding in the two-dimensional sphere, without edge-crossings, of a finite connected planar graph with a distinguished oriented edge.
We allow loops and multiple edges.
We identify two planar maps if there is an orientation-preserving homeomorphism of the sphere that maps one onto the other while matching their root-edges.
Given a map
\mathfrak{m}
, we equip its vertex-set
V(\mathfrak{m})
with the graph distance
d_{\mathfrak{m}}
and the counting measure
\mu_{\mathfrak{m}}=\sum_{v\in V(\mathfrak{m})}\delta_{v}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)
, thus forming a discrete metric measure space
(V(\mathfrak{m}),d_{\mathfrak{m}},\mu_{\mathfrak{m}})
. A
quadrangulation
is a planar map whose faces all have degree
4
.

A major breakthrough in the theory of random planar maps was the proof---by Le Gall
1
1

1

In the same paper, Le Gall also proved the convergence of suitably renormalized
p
-angulations with
n
faces, for
p=3
and all
even

p\geq 4
.

[
33
]

and independently by Miermont
[
41
]
—that the
Brownian sphere
is the scaling limit of uniformly random quadrangulations with
n
faces as
n\to\infty
.
In this paper, we will use their result
2
2

2

To be precise, in
[
33
,
41
]
, the authors show convergence to
(S,d)
in the Gromov–Hausdorff topology, which concerns (isometry classes of) compact metric spaces without mention of measures on them. See the discussion by Le Gall after
[
34
, Thm 7]
on the measure
\mu
and on why the result may be strengthened to GHP convergence to the
measured
Brownian sphere
(S,d,\mu)
. Theorem
1.6
is the GHP version thus obtained.

—reproduced hereafter—as a definition of the Brownian sphere
(S,d,\mu)
, see also Marckert and Mokkadem
[
37
]
for its original definition using the so-called
Brownian snake
, and Miller and Sheffield
[
42
]
for an axiomatic characterization.

Theorem 1.6

(Le Gall, 2013; Miermont, 2013)
.

Letting
Q_{n}
be uniformly random in the set
{\mathcal{Q}}_{n}
of quadrangulations with
n
faces, we have, in distribution for the GHP topology,

\displaystyle\left({V}(Q_{n}),\,\Bigl(\frac{9}{8n}\Bigr)^{1/4}\cdot d_{Q_{n}},\,\frac{1}{n+2}\cdot\mu_{Q_{n}}\right)\xrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{}(S,d,\mu),

where
(S,d,\mu)
is the Brownian sphere.

Convergence to the Brownian sphere has since then been proven for many other models: quadrangulations with no pendant vertices
[
10
]
, general maps
[
11
]
, bipartite maps
[
1
]
, simple quadrangulations/triangulations
[
4
]
, cubic maps
[
18
]
, 2-connected quadrangulations
[
6
]
, odd-angulations
[
5
]
, Eulerian triangulations
[
17
]
, maps with prescribed face degrees
[
38
]
, general/simple cubic planar graphs
[
7
,
48
]
, and Weil–Petersson punctured hyperbolic surfaces
[
13
]
.

We prove a new scaling limit result of this vein, for the model of
irreducible quadrangulations
. A quadrangulation is
irreducible
if it has at least four faces, no multiple edges, and if all its 4-cycles bound a face.

Theorem 1.7
.

Letting
Q^{\mathrm{irr}}_{n}
be uniformly random in the set
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{irr}}_{n}
of irreducible quadrangulations with
n
faces, we have, in distribution for the GHP topology,

\displaystyle\left({V}(Q^{\mathrm{irr}}_{n}),\,\frac{1}{(8n)^{1/4}}\cdot d_{Q^{\mathrm{irr}}_{n}},\,\frac{1}{n+2}\cdot\mu_{Q^{\mathrm{irr}}_{n}}\right)\xrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{(\mathrm{d}),\,\mathrm{GHP}}(S,d,\mu),

where
(S,d,\mu)
is the Brownian sphere.

A complete proof of Theorem
1.7
modulo lemmas is given at the end of Section
2
.
It uses our GHP Tauberian theorem to transfer Le Gall and Miermont’s result (Theorem
1.6
) that
general
quadrangulations converge to the Brownian sphere.
This is accomplished through several largely independent steps: (i)
the condensation phenomenon allows one to find in a large
general
quadrangulation a large
irreducible
one, whose size is random but concentrated, as is known from Gao and Wormald
[
23
]
; (ii) convergence to the Brownian sphere is transferred from the former to the latter
via
robust topological and measure-theoretic arguments; (iii) the Tauberian theorem in GHP space allows to transfer from irreducible quadrangulations with
random
size to
deterministic
size. The
Tauberian assumption
we need to check will follow from the existence of increasing couplings for irreducible quadrangulations of the hexagon—constructed by Addario-Berry
[
3
]
—and a probabilistic argument to “remove the hexagon”.

While the “upwards” movement to derive asymptotics of a
host
model from that of a
component
model has been carried successfully in a number of works on random planar maps—see
[
6
,
8
,
7
,
48
]
for scaling limits and
[
46
,
47
]
for local limits—the converse direction, which requires a means to “condition” a randomized version of the component model, has to the best of our knowledge only been considered in
[
29
]
and
[
12
]
.
In both of these works, a portion of the unconditioned planar map is removed, and then used as a proxy to compare the conditioned and unconditioned versions: in
[
29
]
, Gwynne and Miller rely on fine properties of the peeling process; while in
[
12
]
, Bettinelli, Curien, Fredes, and Sepulveda extract geometric estimates and absolute continuity relations using the known combinatorics of the model.

By contrast, our unconditioning step is an immediate application of Theorem
1.4
, provided that the corresponding stochastic monotonicity assumption holds. The caveat is that the latter condition is arguably non-trivial to check, since it requires to provide couplings between uniformly random maps of size
n
and
n+1
(from the model of interest), in a “distance-increasing” and “measure-increasing” way. Still, Caraceni and Stauffer
[
15
,
16
]
have shown that couplings similar to that of Addario-Berry
[
3
]
exist for a number of other models beyond irreducible quadrangulations.

1.3.
Organisation of the article

In Section
2
, taking for granted the GHP Tauberian theorem (Theorem
1.4
), we decompose our proof of Theorem
1.7
into a few key lemmas, and conclude the proof modulo these lemmas.
The GHP sandwich theorems (Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
) and GHP Tauberian theorem (Theorem
1.4
) are proven in Section
3
, along with various properties of the order
\unlhd
.
Subsequent sections are dedicated to the proof of the key lemmas entering in the proof of Theorem
1.7
. They go as follows: Section
4
elaborates on the condensation phenomenon for irreducible components of general quadrangulations observed by Gao and Wormald
[
23
]
; Section
5
supplies robust topological and measure-theoretic arguments to transfer scaling limit results to “the largest component”, focusing on obtaining the metric (Gromov–Hausdorff) part of the convergence; Section
6
adds the convergence of the measures (Prokhorov part), using ideas of Addario-Berry and Wen
[
6
]
; Section
7
uses couplings by Addario-Berry
[
3
]
to verify the Tauberian assumption on irreducible quadrangulations
of the hexagon
; and lastly in Section
8
, we “get rid of the hexagon”. A concluding section terminates this work.

2.
Structure of the proof of Theorem
1.7

In this section, we take for granted the “GHP Tauberian theorem”, Theorem
1.4
, which will be proved in Section
3
, and jump straight ahead to the description of the key steps in our proof of Theorem
1.7
.

We will repeatedly use the notation
\mathrm{Unif}(F)
or
\mathrm{Unif}_{F}
to denote the uniform probability distribution on a finite non-empty set
F
.

We assume that for every quadrangulation
\mathfrak{q}
, we have fixed some arbitrary total order on the vertex-set, face-set, and edge-set of
\mathfrak{q}
, which we denote by
<_{\mathfrak{q}}
in all four cases.
For instance, such orders can be constructed by performing a breadth-first exploration with clockwise priority, starting from the root oriented edge of
\mathfrak{q}
.

2.1.
Step I: The largest irreducible component

Let
4\leq\ell\leq n
.
In Section
4
, we define a specific set of quadrangulations
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}
, and we consider, for
n,\ell\geq 1
, the set
\mathcal{D}_{n,\ell}
of tuples
(\mathfrak{q}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{q}_{\ell})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}^{\ell}
such that
\sum_{i}|\mathfrak{q}_{i}|=n+\ell
.
We then show that given an irreducible quadrangulation
\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{irr}}\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{irr}}_{\ell}
, a collection
\mathfrak{D}=(\mathfrak{q}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{q}_{\ell})\in\mathcal{D}_{n,\ell}
, and an integer
\mathfrak{j}\in\{1,\dots,4n\}
, we can suitably glue each
\mathfrak{q}_{j}
to the
j
-th face of
\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{irr}}
in
<_{\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{irr}}}
-order.
This gives a quadrangulation
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
rooted at the root-edge of
\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{irr}}
, which allows to define
\mathfrak{q}=\Gamma(\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{irr}};\mathfrak{D},\mathfrak{j})
the re-rooting of
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
at the
\mathfrak{j}
-th oriented edge in
<_{\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}}
-order.

This construction reflects the fact that quadrangulations contain “irreducible components” which can serve as “building blocks” to reconstruct them.
A striking fact with most notions of components in planar maps is that there is usually a unique largest component which gathers a positive and well-concentrated proportion of the mass
[
23
,
9
]
.
We will use this line of ideas to prove the following.

Lemma 2.1
.

There exist random integers
(N_{n})_{n\geq 4}
, and random tuples

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n},\mathfrak{D}_{n},\mathfrak{j}_{n})\qquad\text{in}\qquad{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{irr}}_{N_{n}}\times\mathcal{D}_{n,N_{n}}\times\{1,\dots,4n\}

for
n\geq 4
, such that:
(i) the law of
Q^{\prime}_{n}:=\Gamma(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n};\mathfrak{D}_{n},\mathfrak{j}_{n})
is at
o(1)
total variation distance from
\mathrm{Unif}({\mathcal{Q}}_{n})
as
n\to\infty
; (ii)

\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}
is uniformly distributed in
\smash{{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{irr}}_{N_{n}}}
conditionally on
N_{n}
; (iii) we have the deterministic bound
|K_{n}|\leq n^{2/3+0.1}
where
K_{n}=N_{n}-\lfloor n/9\rfloor
; and, (iv) the collection
\mathfrak{D}_{n}=(\mathfrak{q}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{q}_{N_{n}})\in\mathcal{D}_{n,N_{n}}
is exchangeable conditionally on
N_{n}
.

Our strategy to prove Lemma
2.1
is roughly to condition an
\mathrm{Unif}({\mathcal{Q}}_{n})
-distributed random quadrangulation on having a unique largest irreducible component satisfying the deterministic bound in (iii) above.
Using results of Gao and Wormald
[
23
]
, this amounts to conditioning by an event with probability
1-o(1)
.
The other distributional properties shall then follow easily.
Note that the exponent
2/3+0.1
in the above has been chosen for concreteness.
In fact, Lemma
2.1
still holds if we replace
2/3+0.1
by
2/3+\varepsilon
for any
\varepsilon>0
; and in our proof of Theorem
1.7
, a deterministic bound
|K_{n}|=o(n)
is sufficient.
We prove Lemma
2.1
in Section
4.4
.

2.2.
Step II: A topological/measure-theoretic argument

Our next step is quite general, and completely agnostic to combinatorial details, as it relies on simple topological and measure-theoretic properties of our objects.
Its conclusion can be summarized as follows: if a sequence
(Z_{n})_{n}
of “nice” spaces converges in the GHP sense to a limit
Z_{\infty}
which “has no bottlenecks”; and if for every
n
, the subset
X_{n}\subset Z_{n}
is “not too small” and separated from the rest of
Z_{n}
by bottlenecks; then actually
X_{n}\to Z_{\infty}
in the GHP sense.
In the preceding, “nice” spaces correspond to
compact geodesic metric measure spaces
, the limit “having no bottlenecks” means that it is 2-connected and diffuse (see below), and
X_{n}
being “not too small” means that it has a mass bounded away from
0
.
We refer to Section
5
for definitions.
Let us now make a more precise statement, in Lemma
2.2
below.
For convenience, it is stated with the Hausdorff and Prokhorov metrics, but it can be used to prove Gromov–Hausdorff or Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov convergence, using embedding theorems.

Fix some compact metric space
(Z,\delta)
.
For
n\geq 1
, we let
Z_{n}=X_{n}\cup\bigsqcup_{i\geq 1}Y^{(i)}_{n}
be a compact subspace of
(Z,\delta)
, where
X_{n}
is a non-empty compact subset of
Z_{n}
, and where
\smash{(Y^{(i)}_{n},i\geq 1)}
is a collection of pairwise disjoint and possibly empty compact subsets of
Z_{n}
, each one of them intersecting
X_{n}
.
We suppose that we have chosen some
\smash{y_{n}^{(i)}\in Y^{(i)}_{n}}\cap X_{n}
for every
n,i\geq 1
.
This setup is represented schematically in Figure
1
.

Figure 1.
A schematic representation of the setup of Lemma
2.2
.

On top of that, we let
\mu_{n}
be a finite Borel measure on
Z_{n}
for every
n\geq 1
.
Let us write
\gamma^{(i)}_{n}=\mu_{n}\bigl(Y^{(i)}_{n}\setminus X_{n}\bigr)
, and denote by
\mu^{X}_{n}=\mu_{n}(\cdot\cap X_{n})
the measure
\mu_{n}
restricted to
X_{n}
.
Lastly, define for all
n\geq 1
the measure

(2.1)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)=\mu^{X}_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)+\sum_{i\geq 1}\gamma^{(i)}_{n}\cdot\delta_{y^{(i)}_{n}}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x),

which is supported on
X_{n}
, and which will serve as an approximation of
\mu_{n}
.

The following Lemma
2.2
is a combination of Lemmas
5.7
,
5.8
and
5.9
, which are proven in Section
5
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Suppose that
Z_{n}\to Z_{\infty}
in the
\delta
-Hausdorff metric, that each
Z_{n}
is geodesic, and that
Z_{\infty}
is 2-connected.
Suppose additionally that
\mu_{n}\to\mu_{\infty}
in the
\delta
-Prokhorov metric and that
\mu_{\infty}
is diffuse.
If the following holds

\displaystyle\textstyle\sup_{i\geq 0}\mathrm{diam}(X_{n}\cap Y^{(i)}_{n})\to 0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\textstyle\liminf_{n}\mu_{n}(X_{n})>0,

then
\sup_{i_{\geq}1}\mathrm{diam}(Y^{(i)}_{n})\to 0
, and thus: (i) we have
X_{n}\to Z_{\infty}
in the
\delta
-Hausdorff metric, and (ii) we also have
\widetilde{\mu}_{n}\to\mu_{\infty}
in the
\delta
-Prokhorov metric.
Furthermore, we also have
\smash{\sup_{i\geq 1}\gamma^{(i)}_{n}\to 0}
as
n\to\infty
.

Let us succinctly explain how this is useful in our context.
First, we can turn maps into geodesic spaces using the so-called
metric graph
associated to a graph by replacing its edges by unit segments.
Also, in Lemma
2.1
, the irreducible quadrangulation
\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}
is realized as a submap of
Q^{\prime}_{n}
, in such a way that its metric is the metric induced by
Q^{\prime}_{n}
—that is, informally, that one cannot find shortcuts between points of
\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}
by venturing outside of
\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}
.
This later fact will be easily proven, but it is crucial since it allows to consider the metric graph of
\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}
as a (metric) subspace
X_{n}
of
Z_{n}
—the metric graph of
Q^{\prime}_{n}
.

Once distances are renormalized by
n^{-1/4}
and masses by
n^{-1}
, we are in a similar situation as in Lemma
2.2
, since: (i) we know by Lemma
2.1
that
X_{n}
has (renormalized) mass concentrated around
1/9
and thus bounded away from zero; (ii) the connected components of
Z_{n}\setminus X_{n}
can “touch”
X_{n}
only along a 4-cycle, which has (renormalized) diameter
4\cdot n^{-1/4}\to 0
; and (iii)
Z_{n}
, equipped with the (renormalized) mass measure of
Q^{\prime}_{n}
, converges to the Brownian sphere using the results of Le Gall and Miermont recalled as Theorem
1.6
.
Note that the Brownian sphere is almost surely homeomorphic to the two-dimensional sphere
[
35
,
39
]
and thus 2-connected, and it has diffuse measure by
[
40
, Thm. 3]
.

All in all, we will be able to use Lemma
2.2
to prove Lemma
2.3
.
Let
(N_{n},\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n},\mathfrak{D}_{n})
,
n\geq 4
, be as in Lemma
2.1
.
For
n\geq 4
, if
\mathfrak{D}_{n}=(\mathfrak{q}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{q}_{N_{n}})
, then we set
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)=\sum_{j}\bigl(|V(\mathfrak{q}_{j})|-4\bigr)\cdot\delta_{v_{j}}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)
,
where
v_{j}
is a uniformly chosen vertex incident to the
j
-th face of
\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}
in
<_{\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}}
-order.

Lemma 2.3
.

We have the convergence

\displaystyle\left({V}(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}),\,\left(\frac{9}{8n}\right)^{1/4}\cdot d_{\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}},\,\frac{1}{n+2}\cdot(\mu_{\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}}+\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}})\right)\xrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{(\mathrm{d}),\,\mathrm{GHP}}(S,d,\mu),

where
(S,d,\mu)
is the Brownian sphere.
Furthermore, the collection
(\frac{1}{n+2}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}}(v_{j}),1\leq j\leq N_{n})
is exchangeable conditionally on
N_{n}
, and its supremum converges to
0
in probability as
n\to\infty
.

Lemma
2.3
will be proven in Section
5.4
.

2.3.
Step III: Concentration for exchangeable random measures

In order to control the measure
\frac{1}{n+2}\cdot(\mu_{\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}}+\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}})
in Lemma
2.3
, we will use the following result, which we prove in Section
6
.
It is greatly inspired by arguments of Addario–Berry and Wen
[
6
]
.

We let
(Z,\delta)
be a compact metric space, and we consider a sequence
(D_{n},n\geq 1)
of finite subsets of
Z
.
Suppose that
(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n},n\geq 1)
are
random
measures supported on
D_{n}
respectively.

Proposition 2.4

(See Prop.
6.1
)
.

Assume that the collection
(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}(x))_{x\in D_{n}}
is exchangeable
for every
n\geq 1
.
If
\max_{x\in D_{n}}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}(x)\to 0
in probability, then

\displaystyle\updelta_{\mathrm{P}}\Bigl(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n},|\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}|\cdot\mathrm{Unif}({D_{n}})\Bigr)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0

in probability.

Let again
(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n},\mathfrak{D}_{n})
,
n\geq 4
, be as given by Lemma
2.1
.
Given a map
\mathfrak{m}
, we let
\mathcal{X}(\mathfrak{m})=(V(\mathfrak{m}),d_{\mathfrak{m}},\mu_{\mathfrak{m}})
.
Given a metric measure space
\mathcal{X}=(X,d,\mu)
and
a,b>0
, we write
(a,b)\cdot\mathcal{X}=(X,a\cdot d,b\cdot\mu)
.
By combining Proposition
2.4
and Lemma
2.3
, we will deduce the following.

Lemma 2.5
.

If we set
a_{n}=(9/8n)^{1/4}
and
b_{n}=9/(n+2)
, then we have the convergence:

\displaystyle(a_{n},b_{n})\cdot\mathcal{X}(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n})\xrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{(\mathrm{d}),\,\mathrm{GHP}}(S,d,\mu),

where
(S,d,\mu)
is the Brownian sphere.

Lemma
2.5
is proven in Section
6.3
.

2.4.
Step IV: Stochastic increase for irreducible quadrangulations of the hexagon

As we will see, the convergence in Lemma
2.5
is amenable to the application of the “GHP Tauberian theorem”, Theorem
1.4
. To verify the
Tauberian assumption
, we will need the following stochastic increase statement regarding irreducible quadrangulations
of the hexagon
. The next step will then “remove the hexagon”.

denote the set of
irreducible quadrangulations of the hexagon
with
n
faces, namely planar maps in which (i) the face to the right of the root edge has degree

while all other faces have degree
4
, (ii) there are no multiple edges, and (iii) all 4-cycles bound a face.

Lemma 2.6
.

which only perform a “face-opening” at each step.
Such a coupling has been constructed by Addario–Berry
[
3
]
, using Luczak and Winkler’s increasing coupling for binary trees
[
36
]
, and a bijection by Fusy, Poulalhon and Schaeffer
[
22
]
relating binary trees and irreducible quadrangulations of the hexagon.
There are other models of random planar maps for which such couplings exist, as proven by Caraceni and Stauffer
[
16
]
.

2.5.
Step V: Removing the hexagon

The preceding Lemma
2.6
is not
exactly
what we need to apply the GHP Tauberian theorem, Theorem
1.4
, since it deals with irreducible quadrangulations
of the hexagon
, and not plain irreducible quadrangulations.
In order to compensate for this fact, we prove in Section
8
the following statement.

Lemma 2.7

(See Prop.
8.2
)
.

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}\overset{(\mathrm{d})}{=}Q^{\mathrm{irr}}_{N_{n}},

\displaystyle(a_{n},b_{n})\cdot\mathcal{X}(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n})\xrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{(\mathrm{d}),\,\mathrm{GHP}}(S,d,\mu),

(S,d,\mu)

.

; lastly Assumption 3. corresponds to the stochastic increase established by Lemma

.

, that

.
∎

We now turn to the proof of the intermediate results which have been used in the proof of Theorem
1.7
. The next section deals with general properties of the partial order
\unlhd
, establishing in particular the sandwich theorems, Theorem
1.2
and
1.3
, as well as the GHP Tauberian theorem, Theorem
1.4
, which was used above.

3.
Sandwich and Tauberian theorems in the GHP space

Given some metric space
(X,d)
, we denote by
\mathcal{M}_{f}(X)
, resp.
\mathcal{M}_{1}(X)
, the set of
finite
Borel measures, resp. Borel
probability
measures.

3.1.
The GH and GHP distances

We recall the definition of the Gromov–Hausdorff and Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov distances.
For more details, the reader is referred to
[
14
]
,
[
40
, Section 6]
and
[
2
]
.
We use the definition
5
5

5

To be precise, in
[
2
]
the authors define a GHP distance for
rooted
spaces, but their results adapt
mutatis mutandi
to the unrooted case.

of the GHP distance found in the latter reference, which is an extension to compact spaces endowed with a finite Borel measure of the original definition for spaces endowed with a probability measure.

Let
(Z,\delta)
be a metric space.
For compact subsets
K,K^{\prime}\subset Z
, their
\delta
-
Hausdorff distance
is

\updelta_{\mathrm{H}}(K,K^{\prime})=\inf\bigl\{\varepsilon>0\colon K\subset(K^{\prime})^{\varepsilon}\text{ and }K^{\prime}\subset K^{\varepsilon}\bigr\},

where for every
A\subset Z
and every
\varepsilon>0
we let
A^{\varepsilon}=\{x\in Z:\delta(x,A)<\varepsilon\}
be the
\varepsilon
-neighborhood of
A
in
(Z,\delta)
.
The
\delta
-
Prokhorov distance
between two finite measures
\mu,\mu^{\prime}
on the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}_{Z}
of
(Z,\delta)
is

\updelta_{\mathrm{P}}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})=\inf\bigl\{\varepsilon>0\colon\forall A\in\mathcal{B}_{Z},\,\mu(A)\leq\mu^{\prime}(A^{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon\text{ and }\mu^{\prime}(A)\leq\mu(A^{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon\bigr\}.

The topology on
\mathcal{M}_{f}(Z)
generated by the
\delta
-Prokhorov distance is that of weak convergence (convergence against continuous bounded functions).

The
Gromov–Hausdorff distance
between two (isometry classes of) compact metric spaces
(X,d)
and
(X^{\prime},d^{\prime})
is

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{GH}}\bigl((X,d),(X^{\prime},d^{\prime})\bigr)=\inf_{(Z,\delta),\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}}\updelta_{\mathrm{H}}\bigl(\varphi(X),\varphi^{\prime}(X^{\prime})\bigr),

where the infimum runs over all metric spaces
(Z,\delta)
and all isometric embeddings
\varphi\colon(X,d)\to(Z,\delta)
and
\varphi^{\prime}\colon(X^{\prime},d^{\prime})\to(Z,\delta)
.
This defines a metric on the quotient
\mathbb{K}_{\mathrm{GH}}=\mathbb{K}/\approx
of the space
\mathbb{K}
of compact metric spaces with respect to the relation
\approx
of isometry.

The
Gromov–Hausdorff–Prokhorov distance
between two compact metric measure spaces
\mathcal{X}=(X,d,\mu)
and
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=(X^{\prime},d^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
is

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{GHP}}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}^{\prime})=\inf_{(Z,\delta),\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}}\Bigl(\updelta_{\mathrm{H}}\bigl(\varphi(X),\varphi^{\prime}(X^{\prime})\bigr)+\updelta_{\mathrm{P}}\bigl(\varphi_{*}\mu,\varphi^{\prime}_{*}\mu^{\prime}\bigr)\Bigr),

where again the infimum runs over all metric spaces
(Z,\delta)
and all isometric embeddings
\varphi\colon(X,d)\to(Z,\delta)
and
\varphi^{\prime}\colon(X^{\prime},d^{\prime})\to(Z,\delta)
.
As mentioned in the introduction, we have
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{GHP}}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}^{\prime})=0
precisely when there exists a measure-preserving isometry between
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
, so that this only defines a pseudo-metric on
\mathbb{M}
, which induces an actual metric
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
on the quotient space
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}=\mathbb{M}/{\sim}
with respect to measure-preserving isometry.
The space
(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}},\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{GHP}})
is a Polish space.

3.2.
Correspondences and couplings

There is a useful bound on
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
, which is frequently used in the literature, in terms of
correspondences
and
couplings
.
See
[
40
, Section 6]
for the GHP metric on compact spaces endowed with a Borel probability measure, and
[
34
, Section 2.1]
for the GHP metric on compact spaces endowed with a finite Borel measure, which is our setting.

A correspondence between metric spaces
(X,d)
and
(X^{\prime},d^{\prime})
is a subset
R
of
X\times X^{\prime}
such
that
\pi(R)=X
and
\pi^{\prime}(R)=X^{\prime}
, with the projections
\pi\colon X\times X^{\prime}\to X
and
\pi^{\prime}\colon X\times X^{\prime}\to X^{\prime}
.
The distortion of R is

\displaystyle\mathrm{dis}(R)=\sup\Bigl\{|d(x,y)-d^{\prime}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})|\colon(x,x^{\prime})\in R,\,(y,y^{\prime})\in R\Bigr\}.

Lemma 3.1

(Lemma 4 in
[
34
]
)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and suppose that there is a correspondence
R
between some
(X,d)
and
(X^{\prime},d^{\prime})
with distortion bounded above by
\varepsilon
and a finite measure

\nu
on the product
X\times X^{\prime}
such that
\nu(R^{c})<\varepsilon
and

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{P}}(\pi_{*}\nu,\mu)<\varepsilon,\quad d^{\prime}_{\mathrm{P}}(\pi^{\prime}_{*}\nu,\mu^{\prime})<\varepsilon.

Then
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{GHP}}((X,d,\mu),(X^{\prime},d^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}))<3\varepsilon
.

Given
a,b>0
and
\mathcal{X}=(X,d,\mu)
a compact metric measure space, we write
(a,b)\cdot\mathcal{X}=(X,a\cdot d,b\cdot\mu)
, which is the same space with the distance
d
scaled by
a
and the measure
\mu
scaled by
b
.
We will need the following easy lemma.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
(a_{n},b_{n})\to(1,1)
in
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}
and let
\mathcal{X}_{n}\to\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
in the GHP metric.
Then
(a_{n},b_{n})\cdot\mathcal{X}_{n}\to\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
in the GHP metric.

Proof.

We write
\mathcal{X}_{n}=(X_{n},d_{n},\mu_{n})
for
n\geq 1
and
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}=(X_{\infty},d_{\infty},\mu_{\infty})
.
We let the reader check that for all
n\geq 1
, the hypotheses of Lemma
3.1
are satisfied for

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{n}=2\cdot\max\Bigl(|a_{n}-1|\cdot\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{X}_{n}),|b_{n}-1|\cdot|\mu_{n}|\Bigr);

for
R_{n}
the “diagonal correspondence” between
(a_{n},b_{n})\cdot\mathcal{X}_{n}
and
\mathcal{X}_{n}
given by
R_{n}=\Delta(X_{n})
with
\Delta\colon x\mapsto(x,x)
; and for
\nu_{n}=\Delta_{*}\mu_{n}
.
Hence by Lemma
3.1
,
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{GHP}}((a_{n},b_{n})\cdot\mathcal{X}_{n},\mathcal{X}_{n})\leq 3\varepsilon_{n}
. But
\varepsilon_{n}\to 0
since
(\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{X}_{n}))_{n}
and
(|\mu_{n}|)_{n}
are bounded whenever
(\mathcal{X}_{n})_{n}
converges in the GHP metric, as is easily verified.
∎

For
a,b>0
and
\P(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}})
, we let
(a,b)_{*}\P(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})
be the image measure of
\P(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})
with respect to the mapping
\mathcal{X}\mapsto(a,b)\cdot\mathcal{X}
.

Corollary 3.3
.

Let
(a_{n},b_{n})\to(1,1)
in
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{2}
and let
\P_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\to\P_{\infty}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})
weakly with respect to the GHP topology.
Then
(a_{n},b_{n})_{*}\P_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\to\P_{\infty}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})
weakly with respect to the GHP topology.

Proof.

Let us write
\overline{{\mathbb{N}}}=\{1,2,\dots\}\cup\{\infty\}
.
Since
(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}},\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{GHP}})
is Polish, we may apply the Skhorokhod representation theorem to get a coupling
(\mathcal{X}_{n},n\in\overline{{\mathbb{N}}})
of the distributions
(\P_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X}),n\in\overline{{\mathbb{N}}})
such that
\mathcal{X}_{n}\to\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
almost surely in the GHP metric.
Then
(a_{n},b_{n})\cdot\mathcal{X}_{n}\to\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
almost surely in the GHP metric by Lemma
3.2
.
In particular,
(a_{n},b_{n})_{*}\P_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\to\P_{\infty}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})
weakly with respect to the GHP topology.
∎

3.3.
Isometric embeddings and an Arzela–Ascoli theorem

The following proposition is an extension to the GHP metric of a result for the GH metric proved by Gromov in his seminal paper
[
26
]
, see p. 65 therein.
For similar results with related “Gromov-type” metrics, see also
[
24
, Lem. 5.8 and A.1]
for the Gromov-Hausdorff and Gromov-Prokhorov metrics, and
[
29
, Prop. 1.5]
for the Gromov-Hausdorff-Prokhorov-uniform metric.

The statement and proof of
[
29
, Prop. 1.5]
, for the Gromov-Hausdorff-Prokhorov-uniform (GHPU) metric on compact metric measure spaces endowed with a distinguished continuous curve, adapt
mutatis mutandi
to the GHP metric.
This gives the following statement.

Proposition 3.4

(see
[
29
, Prop. 1.5]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}=(X_{\infty},d_{\infty},\mu_{\infty})
and
\mathcal{X}_{n}=(X_{n},d_{n},\mu_{n})
for
n\geq 1
be elements of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
.
Then
\mathcal{X}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
in the GHP metric if and only if there exists a compact metric space
(Z,\delta)
and isometric embeddings
X_{\infty}\to Z
and
X_{n}\to Z
for
n\geq 1
such that if we identify
X_{\infty}
and
X_{n}
with their images under
these embeddings, then
\updelta_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{n},X_{\infty})\to 0
and
\updelta_{\mathrm{P}}(\mu_{n},\mu_{\infty})\to 0
.

We will also need an Arzela–Ascoli theorem in the context of sequences of compact spaces which converge in the Hausdorff sense
[
28
, Appendix]
.

Let
(Z,\delta)
and
(Z^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
be two compact metric spaces.
For
n\geq 1
, let
f_{n}\colon X_{n}\to X^{\prime}_{n}
, where
X_{n}
and
X^{\prime}_{n}
are compact subsets of
(Z,\delta)
and
(Z^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
respectively.
The sequence
(f_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is said to be
equicontinuous
if for any
\varepsilon>0
, there is a
\eta>0
such that,

\displaystyle\forall x,y\in X_{n},\quad\delta(x,y)<\eta\implies\delta^{\prime}(f_{n}(x),f_{n}(y))<\varepsilon,

for all
n\geq 1
.

Proposition 3.5

(Appendix in
[
28
]
)
.

In this setting, if
X_{n}\to X_{\infty}
in the
\delta
-Hausdorff metric, if
X^{\prime}_{n}\to X^{\prime}_{\infty}
in the
\delta^{\prime}
-Hausdorff metric, and if the sequence
(f_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is equicontinuous, then one can extract a subsequence which converges to a continuous mapping
f_{\infty}\colon X_{\infty}\to X^{\prime}_{\infty}
, in the sense that along that subsequence,
f_{n}(x_{n})\to f_{\infty}(x_{\infty})
in
(Z^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
whenever
x_{n}\to x_{\infty}
in
(Z,\delta)
.

As noted by the authors in
[
28
]
, Proposition
3.5
is proved just as the classical Arzela–Ascoli theorem, making necessary adjustments.
Note that in Gromov’s original paper
[
26
]
, a version of this statement for isometries figures at p. 66.
For a different exposition, see also
[
50
, Thm. 27.20]
.

We will need to complement Proposition
3.5
with the following lemma, which heuristically states that “
(f,\mu)\mapsto\mu\circ f^{-1}
” is upper semi-continuous.

Lemma 3.6
.

In the setting of Proposition
3.5
, if we have finite Borel measures
\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\dots,\mu_{\infty}
on
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{\infty}
respectively such that
\updelta_{\mathrm{P}}(\mu_{n},\mu_{\infty})\to 0
, then for every closed subset
A^{\prime}
of
X^{\prime}_{\infty}
we have

(3.1)

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}(f_{n}^{-1}(A^{\prime}))\leq\mu_{\infty}(f^{-1}_{\infty}(A^{\prime})).

Proof.

Fix some closed subset
A^{\prime}
of
X^{\prime}_{\infty}
.
We claim that for every
\varepsilon>0
we have
f_{n}^{-1}(A^{\prime})\subset(f_{\infty}^{-1}(A^{\prime}))^{\varepsilon}
for all
n\geq 1
large enough.
This implies:

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}(f_{n}^{-1}(A^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\,\limsup_{n\to\infty}\,\mu_{n}\Bigl(\,\overline{(f_{\infty}^{-1}(A^{\prime}))^{\varepsilon}}\,\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\,\mu_{\infty}\Bigl(\,\overline{(f_{\infty}^{-1}(A^{\prime}))^{\varepsilon}}\,\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\infty}(f^{-1}_{\infty}(A^{\prime})),

where for the second line we use the Portmanteau theorem, while the third follows by continuity of measures, since
f^{-1}_{\infty}(A^{\prime})
is closed in
X_{\infty}
by continuity of
f_{\infty}
.

Let us now justify the claim.
Suppose for contradiction that there exists
\varepsilon>0
and infinitely many
n\geq 1
such that we can find
x_{n}\in f_{n}^{-1}(A^{\prime})\setminus(f_{\infty}^{-1}(A^{\prime}))^{\varepsilon}
.
Taking if necessary a subsequence, we may assume that
x_{n}\to x_{\infty}
in the compact
(Z,\delta)
.
Then,
f_{\infty}(x_{\infty})=\lim_{n}f_{n}(x_{n})
is in
A^{\prime}
since
A^{\prime}
is closed in
(Z^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
.
But
\delta(x_{\infty},f_{\infty}^{-1}(A^{\prime}))=\lim_{n}\delta(x_{n},f_{\infty}^{-1}(A^{\prime}))\geq\varepsilon
, hence a contradiction.
∎

Remark 3.7
.

There need not be equality in (
3.1
) since
f_{\infty}^{-1}(A^{\prime})
can be much larger than every
f_{n}^{-1}(A^{\prime})
,
n\geq 1
.
For instance, if
f_{n}\colon[0,1]\to[0,1],\,x\mapsto x/n
for all
n\geq 1
, then
f_{\infty}^{-1}(\{0\})=[0,1]
while
f_{n}^{-1}(\{0\})=\{0\}
for all
n\geq 1
.

3.4.
Properties of the partial order
\unlhd

We recall that the partial order
\unlhd
is defined as follows.
Given
\mathcal{X}=[X,d,\mu]
and
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=[X^{\prime},d^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}]
in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
, we have
\mathcal{X}\unlhd\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
if and only if there exists a
surjective

mapping
\varphi\colon X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
which is

(1)

1-Lipschitz
, that is:

\displaystyle\forall x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in X^{\prime},\ \quad

\displaystyle d\bigl(\varphi(x^{\prime}),\varphi(y^{\prime})\bigr)\leq d^{\prime}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime});

(2)

measure-contracting
, that is:

\displaystyle\forall A\in\mathcal{B},\ \quad

\displaystyle\mu(A)\leq\mu^{\prime}\bigl(\varphi^{-1}(A)\bigr),

where
\mathcal{B}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
(X,d)
.

Let us start with the following lemma.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
(X,d)
be a compact metric space.
If a surjective self-mapping
\psi\colon X\to X
is 1-Lipschitz and measure-contracting, then it is a measure-preserving isometry.

Proof.

It is classical that a 1-Lipschitz surjective self-mapping is a (bijective) isometry, see
e.g.

[
14
, Thm. 1.6.15]
.
Let
\psi
be a surjective and measure-
contracting
self-mapping
\psi\colon X\to X
, and let us show that it is actually measure-
preserving
.
For every
A
in the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}
of
X
, its complement
A^{c}
is also in
\mathcal{B}
, and using that
\psi
is measure-contracting we have

\displaystyle\mu(A)\leq\mu\bigl(\psi^{-1}(A)\bigr)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mu(A^{c})\leq\mu\bigl(\psi^{-1}(A^{c})\bigr).

Since
\psi^{-1}(A^{c})
is the complement of
\psi^{-1}(A)
in
X
, we have
\mu(\psi^{-1}(A))+\mu(\psi^{-1}(A^{c}))=|\mu|=\mu(A)+\mu(A^{c})
.
Hence the above inequalities must be equalities, so that
\mu(A)=\mu(\psi^{-1}(A))
and
\psi
is measure-preserving.
∎

Proposition 3.9
.

The relation
\unlhd
is a partial order on
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
.

Proof.

The transitivity and reflexivity of
\unlhd
are clear.
What is needed for
\unlhd
to be a partial order is therefore its anti-symmetry.
This amounts to proving that for
\mathcal{X}=(X,d,\mu)
and
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=(X^{\prime},d^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
two compact metric measure spaces, if
\mathcal{X}\unlhd\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\unlhd\mathcal{X}
then
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
are measure-preserving isometric.
Hence let
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
be such that
\mathcal{X}\unlhd\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\unlhd\mathcal{X}
, with respective Borel
\sigma
-algebras
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
.
By definition, there exist surjective mappings
\varphi\colon X^{\prime}\to X
and
\varphi^{\prime}\colon X\to X^{\prime}
such that:

\left\{\begin{matrix}[l]\forall x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in X^{\prime},\quad&d(\varphi(x^{\prime}),\varphi(y^{\prime}))\leq d^{\prime}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}),\\
\forall A\in\mathcal{B},\quad&\mu(A)\leq\mu^{\prime}(\varphi^{-1}(A)),\end{matrix}\right.\\
\text{and}\qquad\left\{\begin{matrix}[l]\forall x,y\in X,\quad&d^{\prime}(\varphi^{\prime}(x),\varphi^{\prime}(y))\leq d(x,y),\\
\forall A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime},\quad&\mu^{\prime}(A^{\prime})\leq\mu((\varphi^{\prime})^{-1}(A^{\prime})).\end{matrix}\right.

In particular, the self-mapping
\psi\colon X\to X
defined by
\psi=\varphi\circ\varphi^{\prime}
satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
3.8
and it is therefore a measure-preserving isometry.
Hence the inequalities in the last display must be equalities and
\varphi
and
\varphi^{\prime}
are measure-preserving isometries.
∎

The following properties are useful in order to prove a sandwich theorem, see Lemma
3.14
.

Definition 3.10
.

Let
\preceq
be a partial order on some metric space
\mathbb{X}
.
It is said to be:

(1)

closed
if its graph
\{(x,y)\in X\colon x\preceq y\}
is closed in
\mathbb{X}
,

(2)

down-compact
if given any compact
K^{+}\subset\mathbb{X}
, the set

\mathrm{Down}_{\preceq}(K^{+})=\{x\in\mathbb{X}\colon\exists x^{+}\in K^{+},\,x\preceq x^{+}\}

is also compact.

We now verify that the order
\unlhd
is indeed closed and down-compact.

Proposition 3.11
.

The partial order
\unlhd
on
(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}},\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{GHP}})
is closed.

Proof.

We have to prove that if
\mathcal{X}_{n}\to\mathcal{X}_{\infty}
and
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{n}\to\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{\infty}
in the GHP metric, and if
\mathcal{X}_{n}\unlhd\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{n}
for all
n
, then
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\unlhd\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{\infty}
.

By Proposition
3.4
, we only need to treat the case where there are two compact metric spaces
(Z,\delta)
and
(Z^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
such that
X_{\infty}
and
X_{n}
are subsets of
(Z,\delta)
equipped with the induced metric,
X^{\prime}_{\infty}
and
X^{\prime}_{n}
are subsets of
(Z^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
equipped with the induced metric, and
\updelta_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{n},X_{\infty})\to 0
,
\updelta_{\mathrm{P}}(\mu_{n},\mu_{\infty})\to 0
,
\updelta_{\mathrm{H}}^{\prime}(X^{\prime}_{n},X^{\prime}_{\infty})\to 0
, and
\updelta_{\mathrm{P}}^{\prime}(\mu^{\prime}_{n},\mu^{\prime}_{\infty})\to 0
.

Let us therefore consider that this is the case, and for every
n\geq 1
assume that we have
\mathcal{X}_{n}\unlhd\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{n}
,
i.e.
there is a surjective mapping
\varphi_{n}\colon X^{\prime}_{n}\to X_{n}
, such that

(3.2)

\displaystyle\forall x^{\prime}_{n},y^{\prime}_{n}\in X^{\prime}_{n},\ \qquad

\displaystyle\delta(\varphi_{n}(x^{\prime}_{n}),\varphi_{n}(y^{\prime}_{n}))\leq\delta^{\prime}(x^{\prime}_{n},y^{\prime}_{n})

(3.3)

\displaystyle\forall A\in\mathcal{B}_{n},\ \qquad

\displaystyle\mu_{n}(A)\leq\mu^{\prime}_{n}(\varphi^{-1}_{n}(A)),

where
\mathcal{B}_{n}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
(X_{n},d_{n})
.
We need to prove that
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\unlhd\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{\infty}
.

The condition (
3.2
) directly implies that the sequence
(\varphi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is equicontinuous,
6
6

6

Here the roles of “
X
” and “
X^{\prime}
” are reversed compared to how we defined equicontinuity, since we consider “
X^{\prime}\to X
” mappings.

so that by Proposition
3.5
, up to taking a subsequence we may assume that
(\varphi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
converges, to some limit
\varphi_{\infty}\colon X^{\prime}_{\infty}\rightarrow X_{\infty}
.
The meaning of this convergence, as given by Proposition
3.5
, is that
\varphi_{n}(x^{\prime}_{n})\to\varphi_{\infty}(x^{\prime}_{\infty})
in
(Z,\delta)
whenever
x^{\prime}_{n}\to x^{\prime}_{\infty}
in
(Z^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
.

We claim that
\varphi_{\infty}\colon X^{\prime}_{\infty}\to X_{\infty}
is surjective.
Let
x_{\infty}\in X_{\infty}
.
Since
X_{n}\to X_{\infty}
in the Hausdorff metric, we may find elements
x_{n}\in X_{n}
,
n\geq 1
, such that
x_{n}\to x_{\infty}
.
For
n\geq 1
, by surjectivity of
\varphi_{n}
we may find
x^{\prime}_{n}\in X^{\prime}_{n}
such that
\varphi_{n}(x^{\prime}_{n})=x_{n}
.
Since
(Z^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
is compact, after possibly taking a subsequence, we have
x^{\prime}_{n}\to x^{\prime}_{\infty}
for some element
x^{\prime}_{\infty}\in Z^{\prime}
, which is actually an element of
X^{\prime}_{\infty}
since
X^{\prime}_{n}\to X^{\prime}_{\infty}
in the Hausdorff metric.
Lastly,
\varphi_{\infty}(x^{\prime}_{\infty})=\lim_{n}\varphi_{n}(x^{\prime}_{n})=\lim_{n}x_{n}=x_{\infty}
, so that
x_{\infty}\in\varphi_{\infty}(X^{\prime}_{\infty})
.
This proves that
\varphi_{\infty}\colon X^{\prime}_{\infty}\to X_{\infty}
is indeed surjective.

We claim that:

(3.4)

\displaystyle\forall x^{\prime}_{\infty},y^{\prime}_{\infty}\in X^{\prime}_{\infty},\ \qquad

\displaystyle\delta(\varphi_{\infty}(x^{\prime}_{\infty}),\varphi_{\infty}(y^{\prime}_{\infty}))\leq\delta^{\prime}(x^{\prime}_{\infty},y^{\prime}_{\infty})

(3.5)

\displaystyle\forall A\in\mathcal{B}_{\infty},\ \qquad

\displaystyle\mu_{\infty}(A)\leq\mu^{\prime}_{\infty}(\varphi^{-1}_{\infty}(A)),

where
\mathcal{B}_{\infty}
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
X_{\infty}
equipped with the metric induced by
\delta
.
Once proven, this precisely tells that
\mathcal{X}_{\infty}\unlhd\mathcal{X}^{\prime}_{\infty}
and the proof is complete.

Let us prove this claim.
Since
\varphi_{n}(x^{\prime}_{n})\to\varphi_{\infty}(x^{\prime}_{\infty})
in
(Z,\delta)
whenever
x^{\prime}_{n}\to x^{\prime}_{\infty}
in
(Z^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
, we obtain (
3.4
) from (
3.2
) by taking suitable convergent sequences
x^{\prime}_{n}\to x^{\prime}_{\infty}
and
y^{\prime}_{n}\to y^{\prime}_{\infty}
, as in the proof of the surjectivity of
\varphi_{\infty}
.
Since
\mu_{\infty}
is a finite Borel measure on the compact
X_{\infty}
, it is inner regular.
Hence we only need to check (
3.5
) for subsets
A
of
X_{\infty}
which are
compact
. It is sufficient
7
7

7

Let
A
be any compact in
X_{\infty}
and fix some
x\in X_{\infty}
. The boundaries in
X_{\infty}
of the sets
\bigl(A\cap B(x,r),r>0\bigr)
are disjoint, and
\mu_{\infty}
is a finite measure, so that except in finitely many instances, the boundaries of the sets
\bigl(A\cap B(x,r),r>0\bigr)
have zero
\mu_{\infty}
-measure. We can therefore apply the reasoning to them and conclude by continuity of measure.

to consider compacts
A
whose boundary in
X_{\infty}
have zero
\mu_{\infty}
-measure.
For such an
A
, we have
\mu_{n}(A)\to\mu_{\infty}(A)
by the Portmanteau theorem, while Lemma
3.6
tells us that
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mu^{\prime}_{n}(\varphi^{-1}_{n}(A))\leq\mu^{\prime}_{\infty}(\varphi^{-1}_{\infty}(A))
.
Therefore, taking the limit superior on both sides of (
3.3
) yields (
3.5
).
∎

Proposition 3.12
.

The partial order
\unlhd
on
(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}},\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{GHP}})
is down-compact.

Proof.

By Theorem 2.6 in
[
2
]
, a subset
A\subset\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
is relatively compact if and only if
8
8

8

[
2
, Thm. 2.6]
only says that these conditions are sufficient, but it is easily verified that they are necessary.
For instance, if there exists a sequence of elements of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
violating one of three conditions, then one can extract a converging subsequence and reason as in
[
2
, Sect. 4.2]
.

the following three conditions hold:

(1)

we have
\sup_{(X,d,\mu)\in A}\,\mathrm{diam}(X)<\infty
;

(2)

for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a finite integer
N_{\varepsilon}\geq 1
such that for
(X,d,\mu)\in A
, there is a covering of
X
by at most
N_{\varepsilon}
balls with radius less than
\varepsilon
; and,

(3)

we have
\sup_{(X,d,\mu)\in A}\,\mu(X)<\infty
.

The crucial observation is that if
A\subset\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
satisfies the above conditions, then so does
\mathrm{Down}_{\unlhd}(A)=\{\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}\colon\exists\mathcal{X}^{+}\in A,\,\mathcal{X}\unlhd\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\}
, as is clear from the definition of the order
\unlhd
.
In particular, if
K^{+}\subset\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
is compact, then
\mathrm{Down}_{\unlhd}(K^{+})
is relatively compact in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
; and using Proposition
3.11
, it is also closed in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
.
Hence,
\mathrm{Down}_{\unlhd}(K^{+})
is compact in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
, as needed.
∎

We conclude this section with the following immediate yet useful lemma.

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
a,b>0
.

(1)

We have
(a,b)\cdot\mathcal{X}\unlhd(a,b)\cdot\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
whenever
\mathcal{X}\unlhd\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
.

(2)

We have
(a,b)_{*}\P(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\unlhd_{\mathrm{st}}(a,b)_{*}\P^{\prime}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})
whenever
\P(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\unlhd_{\mathrm{st}}\P^{\prime}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})
in
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}})
.

3.5.
Two lemmas on sandwich theorems

Lemma 3.14
.

If
\mathbb{X}
is a metric space and
\preceq
is a closed down-compact partial order on it, then we have the following sandwich theorem:

(3.6)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\forall n,\quad{x}^{-}_{n}\preceq{x}_{n}\preceq{x}^{+}_{n},\\
{x}^{-}_{n}\to{x}_{\infty},\\
{x}^{+}_{n}\to{x}_{\infty},\end{cases}\qquad\implies\qquad{x}_{n}\to{x}_{\infty},

for all sequences
({x}^{-}_{n})_{n\geq 1},({x}_{n})_{n\geq 1},({x}^{+}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
of elements of
\mathbb{X}
, and all
{x}_{\infty}\in\mathbb{X}
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove that every subsequence of
({x}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
admits a subsequence converging to
{x}_{\infty}
.
Since
{x}^{+}_{n}\to{x}_{\infty}
, the subset
K^{+}=\{{x}^{+}_{1},{x}^{+}_{2},\dots\}\cup\{{x}_{\infty}\}
of
\mathbb{X}
is compact, and thus
\mathrm{Down}_{\preceq}(K^{+})
is compact too, using the down-compactness of
\preceq
.
But
({x}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
takes values in
\mathrm{Down}_{\preceq}(K^{+})
, so that every subsequence of
({x}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
admits a converging subsequence, say with limit
{x}^{\prime}_{\infty}
.
It remains to prove that necessarily
{x}^{\prime}_{\infty}={x}_{\infty}
.
But since
\preceq
is closed we must have
{x}_{\infty}\preceq{x}^{\prime}_{\infty}\preceq{x}_{\infty}
and therefore
{x}^{\prime}_{\infty}={x}_{\infty}
since
\preceq
is a partial order.
∎

Lemma 3.15
.

If
\mathbb{X}
is a Polish space and
\preceq
is a closed down-compact partial order on it, then
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{X})
is also Polish and
\preceq_{\mathrm{st}}
is also a closed down-compact partial order.

Proof.

First, if
\mathbb{X}
is Polish and
\preceq
is a closed partial order, then
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{X})
is also Polish and
\preceq_{\mathrm{st}}
is also a closed partial order by
[
32
, Thm. 2]
.
Now suppose that
\preceq
is down-compact, and let us prove that
\preceq_{\mathrm{st}}
is also down-compact.

Let
\mathcal{K}^{+}
be an arbitrary compact subset of
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{X})
, and fix some
\varepsilon>0
.
By Prokhorov’s theorem,
\mathcal{K}^{+}
is a tight collection in
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{X})
, so that there exists some compact subset
K^{+}_{\varepsilon}\subset\mathbb{X}
such that

(3.7)

\displaystyle\sup_{\P^{+}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{x})\in\mathcal{K}^{+}}\P^{+}(\mathbb{X}\setminus K^{+}_{\varepsilon})\leq\varepsilon.

Let
\P(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{x})\in\mathrm{Down}_{\preceq_{\mathrm{st}}}(\mathcal{K}^{+})
, which means by definition that
\P(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{x})\preceq_{\mathrm{st}}\P^{+}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{x})
for some
\P^{+}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{x})\in\mathcal{K}^{+}
.
Since
\preceq
is down-compact, we have that
D_{\varepsilon}=\mathrm{Down}_{\preceq}(K^{+}_{\varepsilon})
is a
compact
subset of
\mathbb{X}
.
By construction,
\mathbb{X}\setminus D_{\varepsilon}
is an
\preceq
-increasing Borel event on
\mathbb{X}
.
Since
\P(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{x})\preceq_{\mathrm{st}}\P^{+}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{x})
, this implies:

\displaystyle\P(\mathbb{X}\setminus D_{\varepsilon})\leq\P^{+}(\mathbb{X}\setminus D_{\varepsilon})\leq\P^{+}(\mathbb{X}\setminus K^{+}_{\varepsilon})\leq\varepsilon,

where the second inequality comes from the inclusion
K^{+}_{\varepsilon}\subset D_{\varepsilon}
, and the last one uses (
3.7
).
Hence we just proved that for every
\varepsilon>0
we can find a compact subset
D_{\varepsilon}\subset\mathbb{X}
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\P(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}{x})\in\mathrm{Down}_{\preceq_{\mathrm{st}}}({\mathcal{K}^{+}})}\P(X\setminus D_{\varepsilon})\leq\varepsilon.

That is, we proved that
\mathrm{Down}_{\preceq_{\mathrm{st}}}({\mathcal{K}^{+}})
is a tight collection in
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{X})
, and therefore it is pre-compact by Prokhorov’s theorem.
On top of being pre-compact,
\mathrm{Down}_{\preceq_{\mathrm{st}}}(\mathcal{K}^{+})
is also closed (and hence compact) in
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{X})
.
This is easily seen from the definition of
\mathrm{Down}_{\preceq_{\mathrm{st}}}
, using that
\preceq_{\mathrm{st}}
is a closed partial order and that
\mathcal{K}^{+}
is closed in
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{X})
.

Wrapping up, we proved that for every compact subset
\mathcal{K}^{+}\subset\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{X})
, the set
\mathrm{Down}_{\preceq_{\mathrm{st}}}(\mathcal{K}^{+})
is also a compact subset of
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{X})
.
Hence,
\preceq_{\mathrm{st}}
is down-compact, as needed.
∎

3.6.
Proofs of the GHP sandwich and Tauberian theorems

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

The binary relation
\unlhd
is a partial order on
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}}
by Proposition
3.9
, it is closed by Proposition
3.11
, and it is down-compact by Proposition
3.12
.
We conclude with Lemma
3.14
that the claimed sandwich theorem is satisfied.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Since
(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}},\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{GHP}})
is Polish
[
2
, Thm. 2.5]
, and
\unlhd
is a closed down-compact partial order on it by Theorem
1.2
, we deduce using Lemma
3.14
that
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{GHP}})
is also Polish and
\unlhd_{\mathrm{st}}
is also a closed down-compact partial order.
Again, we conclude with Lemma
3.14
that the claimed sandwich theorem is satisfied.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

We recall the setting of Theorem
1.4
: we have random compact metric measure spaces
\left(\mathfrak{X}_{n},n\geq 1\right)
and
\mathfrak{X}_{\infty}
, two tame sequences
(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}
,
(b_{n})_{n\geq 1}
, and random variables
(N_{n})_{n\geq 1}
independent of
(\mathfrak{X}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and
\mathfrak{X}_{\infty}
such that:

(1)

There holds
(a_{n},b_{n})\cdot\mathfrak{X}_{N_{n}}\to\mathfrak{X}_{\infty}
in distribution for the GHP topology.

(2)

There holds
N_{n}/n\rightarrow 1
in probability.

(3)

We have
\P_{1}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\unlhd_{\mathrm{st}}\P_{2}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\unlhd_{\mathrm{st}}\P_{3}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\unlhd_{\mathrm{st}}\dots
, where
\P_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})
is the distribution of
\mathfrak{X}_{n}
for every
n\geq 1
.

Since
N_{n}/n\rightarrow 1
in probability, we can find
9
9

9

For instance, set
k_{n}=\lfloor{n\varepsilon_{n}}\rfloor
where
\varepsilon_{n}
is the infimal
\varepsilon
such that the probability that
N_{n-\lfloor{n\varepsilon}\rfloor}<n<N_{n+\lfloor{n\varepsilon}\rfloor}
is at least
1-\varepsilon
.

positive integers
k_{n}=o(n)
such that
\P\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\,N_{n-k_{n}}<n\,}}\right)\to 1
and
\P\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\,N_{n+k_{n}}>n\,}}\right)\to 1
.
In particular, the random variables
L^{-}_{n}=\min(n,N_{n-k_{n}})
and
L^{+}_{n}=\max(n,N_{n+k_{n}})
are at vanishing total variation distance from
N_{n-k_{n}}
and
N_{n+k_{n}}
respectively.
Hence we can replace
N_{n\pm k_{n}}
by
L^{\pm}_{n}
in the convergence
(a_{n\pm k_{n}},b_{n\pm k_{n}})\cdot\mathfrak{X}_{N_{n\pm k_{n}}}\to\mathfrak{X}_{\infty}
which follows from (1) above: this gives
(a_{n\pm k_{n}},b_{n\pm k_{n}})\cdot\mathfrak{X}_{L^{\pm}_{n}}\to\mathfrak{X}_{\infty}
after replacement.
Now, since
(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and
(b_{n})_{n\geq 1}
are tame and
k_{n}=o(n)
, we have
a_{n\pm k_{n}}\sim a_{n}
and
b_{n\pm k_{n}}\sim b_{n}
, so that by Corollary
3.3
, we deduce that
(a_{n},b_{n})\cdot\mathfrak{X}_{L^{\pm}_{n}}\to\mathfrak{X}_{\infty}
in distribution for the GHP topology.
Hence we have shown that

(3.8)

\displaystyle(a_{n},b_{n})_{*}\P^{\pm}_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\xRightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{\mathrm{GHP}}\P_{\infty}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X}),

where
\P^{\pm}_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})
is the distribution of
\mathfrak{X}_{L^{\pm}_{n}}
for every
n\geq 1
.
For all
n\geq 1
, the distribution
\P^{-}_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})
is a mixture of
(\P_{k}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X}),k\leq n)
while
\P^{+}_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})
is a mixture of
(\P_{k}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X}),k\geq n)
.
Hence (3) above gives:

\displaystyle\P^{-}_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\unlhd_{\mathrm{st}}\P_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\unlhd_{\mathrm{st}}\P^{+}_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X}).

Since the order
\unlhd_{\mathrm{st}}
is scale-invariant in the sense of Lemma
3.13
, we get

(3.9)

\displaystyle(a_{n},b_{n})_{*}\P^{-}_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\unlhd_{\mathrm{st}}(a_{n},b_{n})_{*}\P_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\unlhd_{\mathrm{st}}(a_{n},b_{n})_{*}\P^{+}_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X}).

Finally, from (
3.8
) and (
3.9
), the sandwich theorem Theorem
1.3
yields that:

\displaystyle(a_{n},b_{n})_{*}\P_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X})\xRightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{\mathrm{GHP}}\P_{\infty}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathcal{X}),

which means that
(a_{n},b_{n})\cdot\mathfrak{X}_{n}\to\mathfrak{X}_{\infty}
in distribution for the GHP topology.
∎

This concludes the present section regarding the partial order
\unlhd
. We have established in particular the GHP Tauberian theorem, Theorem
1.4
, which serves as the main tool behind the proof of Theorem
1.7
on the scaling limit of irreducible quadranagulations. The proof of the latter theorem was given in Section
2
up to some some key lemmas, which we now address. In the next section, we begin with the first step: finding a large
irreducible component
in a large
general quadrangulation
, in order to prove Lemma
2.1
.

4.
Step I: The largest irreducible component

Given some combinatorial object or algebraic structure, there are often two dual ways to make sense of what “an object found in another object” means, namely by considering either
sub-objects
or
quotients
.

Informally, for us a “sub-object” of a planar map
\mathfrak{m}
will correspond to a “map embedded in
\mathfrak{m}
”, that is a
submap
as defined in Section
4.1
; and a “quotient” of a planar map
\mathfrak{m}
will be any planar map that we can obtain by removing/collapsing some parts of
\mathfrak{m}
.
This will give us two notions of what the “largest irreducible quadrangulation” found in some quadrangulation
\mathfrak{q}
means:

(1)

The largest
irreducible component
, where an irreducible component is a submap which is irreducible and satisfies an additional condition which guarantees uniqueness properties.

(2)

The largest
irreducible block
, where an irreducible block is obtained informally by collapsing the sides of the 2-cycles which do not contain some fixed edge, and then emptying the “sides of the 4-cycles” which do not contain that edge.

Definitions vary among authors, and the words “component” and “block” are often used interchangeably, mainly to describe what we call a “block”.
Sometimes there is a clear choice of embedding which turns the block into an actual submap (for instance 2-connected blocks in general maps).
One of the aims of this section is to clarify the links between the two notions, explaining in particular why the
blocks
considered in the literature can be canonically realized as submaps, which is convenient for metric comparisons between a quadrangulation and its largest irreducible block.

After some preliminaries, we define the notion of irreducible
components
we will consider.
Then, we show that it leads to the same objects as the notion of
blocks
mentioned above.
This will allow us to use results of Gao and Wormald
[
23
]
on block-sizes, and in particular to deduce a
condensation
phenomenon: asymptotically, in a uniformly random quadrangulation with
n
faces, there is a unique largest component with approximately
n/9
faces.
Lastly, we complement this result with some distributional properties of the largest irreducible component.

4.1.
Preliminaries on maps and submaps

Let us first recall some definitions from the introduction. A
planar map
is a proper embedding in the two-dimensional sphere, without edge-crossings, of a finite connected planar graph with a distinguished oriented edge. Loops and multiple edges are allowed and two planar maps are identified if there is an orientation-preserving homeomorphism of the sphere that maps one onto the other while matching their root-edges.

We recall that a
quadrangulation
is a planar map in which all faces have degree four. It is
simple
if it has no 2-cycle, and it is
irreducible
if it is simple, has at least four faces, is such that all 4-cycles bound a face.

Sides of a simple cycle

We will repeatedly speak about the “sides” of a simple cycle, so let us give a formal definition once and for all.
In a planar map, a
k
-cycle (reps. oriented
k
-cycle) is a path (resp. oriented path) of length
k
which ends at its starting vertex, and it is said to be a
simple cycle
if it uses any other vertex at most once.

Consider some planar
\mathfrak{m}
, and let us fix an embedding of it in the 2-sphere.
Under this embedding, every simple cycle
C
in
\mathfrak{m}
corresponds to a Jordan curve, separating the 2-sphere into two simply connected regions whose closures are, say,
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
respectively.
We call these the
sides
of
C
.
We then say that two faces/edges/vertices/paths/…of
\mathfrak{m}
are
on the same side
of
C
if the embedding sends them both in
R_{1}
or both in
R_{2}
.
In particular, the only edges and vertices which are on both sides are the ones which belong to
C
.
Note that the relation of “being on the same side” does not actually depend on the choice of embedding (in its orientation-preserving homeomorphism class).

Given an
oriented
simple cycle
\vec{C}
, its
interior side
, or simply its
interior
, is the side of
\vec{C}
around which
\vec{C}
runs counter-clockwise.
The other side is called the
exterior side
, or simply the
exterior
of
\vec{C}
.
By convention, the
oriented boundary

\vec{\partial}f
of a face
f
with simple boundary in some map
\mathfrak{m}
is the oriented cycle whose interior is that face.

Submaps

A submap of a planar map
\mathfrak{m}
is a pair
(\mathfrak{s},\alpha)
where
\mathfrak{s}
is a rooted planar map and
\alpha\colon V(\mathfrak{s})\sqcup E(\mathfrak{s})\to V(\mathfrak{m})\sqcup E(\mathfrak{m})
is an injective mapping sending vertices/edges of
\mathfrak{s}
to vertices/edges of
\mathfrak{m}
respectively, in such a way that any embedding of
\mathfrak{m}
in the sphere induces through
\alpha
a proper embedding of the underlying graph of
\mathfrak{s}
which is precisely in the orientation-preserving homeomorphism class characterizing
\mathfrak{s}
.
Informally, this just means that
\alpha
describes a graph homomorphism which is compatible with the embeddings prescribed by
\mathfrak{s}
and
\mathfrak{m}
.
In a slightly abusive way, we will often drop mention of the mapping
\alpha
and identify the vertices/edges of
\mathfrak{s}
with the vertices/edges of
\mathfrak{m}
they are mapped to
via
the mapping
\alpha
.

We will say that two submaps
\mathfrak{s}
and
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
of a planar map
\mathfrak{m}
are
equivalent up to re-rooting
, or
re-rooting equivalent
, if we have
E(\mathfrak{s})=E(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})
as subsets of
E(\mathfrak{m})
.
Note however that, in this case, the oriented root edges of
\mathfrak{s}
and
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
—seen as oriented edges of
\mathfrak{m}
—might be distinct.

Detaching and gluing

Suppose we are given some algorithm
\psi
which to a map
\mathfrak{m}
and a face
f
on it associates an oriented edge
\psi(\mathfrak{m};f)
on the oriented cycle
\vec{\partial}f
.
It will be implicit in notation.
Let
(\mathfrak{s},\alpha)
be a submap of a map
\mathfrak{m}
.
Suppose that
\mathfrak{s}
has
\ell\geq 2
faces, all of them with a
simple boundary
, and enumerate them as
(f_{1},\dots,f_{\ell})
using the order
<_{\mathfrak{s}}
.
For
i=1,\dots,\ell
, the interior of
\vec{\partial}f_{i}
can be rooted at
\psi(\mathfrak{s};f_{i})
.
This yields, using any proper embedding of
\mathfrak{m}
, a rooted planar map
\mathfrak{m}_{i}=\mathfrak{m}_{i}(\mathfrak{m};\mathfrak{s},\alpha)
.
We can thus form the collection
\mathfrak{D}=\mathfrak{D}(\mathfrak{m};\mathfrak{s},\alpha)
of rooted maps by setting
\mathfrak{D}=(\mathfrak{m}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq\ell}
.
Lastly, we let
\mathfrak{j}=\mathfrak{j}(\mathfrak{m};\mathfrak{s},\alpha)\in\{1,\dots,4|\mathfrak{m}|\}
be the index of the root edge of
\mathfrak{m}
in the order
<_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}
, where
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
is
\mathfrak{m}
re-rooted at the root edge of
\mathfrak{s}
.
All in all, we have defined a triple
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{D},\mathfrak{j})
, which is a function of
(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{s},\alpha)
.
Hence we may define:

\displaystyle\Psi(\mathfrak{m};\mathfrak{s},\alpha)=(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{D},\mathfrak{j}).

There is an inverse operation.
Consider given a planar map
\mathfrak{s}
whose faces
(f_{1},\dots,f_{\ell})
—in the order
<_{\mathfrak{s}}
—have a simple boundary, a collection
\mathfrak{D}=(\mathfrak{m}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq\ell}
of rooted planar maps whose root faces have a simple boundary of length equal to that of
\vec{\partial}f_{i}
respectively, and an integer
1\leq\mathfrak{j}\leq 4\sum_{i}(|\mathfrak{m}_{i}|-1)
.
Then, one easily reverses the operation described above, so as to “glue back” the collection of maps of
\mathfrak{D}
in order to realize a rooted map
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}
and a submap
(\mathfrak{s},\alpha)
satisfying that
\Psi(\mathfrak{m};\mathfrak{s},\alpha)=(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{D},\mathfrak{j})
.
We write:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Phi}(\mathfrak{s};\mathfrak{D},\mathfrak{j})

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{s})

\displaystyle\Gamma(\mathfrak{s};\mathfrak{D},\mathfrak{j})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{m}.

Remark 4.1
.

The “detaching” and “gluing” operations can still be defined if we relax the assumption that the submap
\mathfrak{s}
we consider is such that all its faces have a simple boundary.
This requires to extend the notion of “sides of a cycle” to cycles which are only non-crossing.
However, we will not need this level of generality.

4.2.
Irreducible submaps and components

Irreducibility (as defined in the introduction) is in some sense a strengthening of the notion of “connectedness”: informally, a quadrangulation is irreducible if it not possible to partition it non-trivially into two pieces whose intersection is a 4-cycle; whereas a space is connected if it cannot be non-trivially partitioned into two pieces whose intersection is the empty set.
Hence, just as connected components are maximal connected subsets in a topological space, it is to be expected that there is a well-defined notion of “irreducible components”.
It is tempting to declare that an irreducible component is just a maximal irreducible submap, with respect to inclusion.
But in order to enforce desirable uniqueness properties, we will need to consider “rightmost” irreducible submaps, as we now define.

Canonical orientation and rightmost cycles

Let
\mathfrak{q}
be a quadrangulation.
Since its faces has even degrees, it is bipartite, that is it admits a
proper bi-coloring
: a coloring of each vertex with black or white such that no two neighbor vertices have the same color.
If we require the root oriented edge of
\mathfrak{q}
to be oriented black-to-white, then there is a unique such proper bi-coloring; which is easily constructed by a greedy exploration starting from the root edge.
The obtained bi-coloring of
\mathfrak{q}
will be called the
canonical bi-coloring
, and when there is no confusion possible, we will simply speak about
black
and
white
vertices of
\mathfrak{q}
in reference to their color under the canonical bi-coloring.
The black-to-white orientation
\vec{e}
of a non-oriented edge
e
of
\mathfrak{q}
will be called the
canonical orientation
of
e
.

Let
C
be a simple cycle on
\mathfrak{q}
.
We will say that it is a
rightmost cycle
if the following holds: for every edge
e
on
C
, say between some black vertex
v_{\bullet}
and some white vertex
v_{\circ}
, all the edges joining
v_{\bullet}
to
v_{\circ}
belong to the side of
C
which sits to the left of
e
, when oriented in the canonical black-to-white orientation.
Accordingly, a submap
\mathfrak{s}
of the quadrangulation
\mathfrak{q}
is said to be a
rightmost submap
if its faces are bordered by cycles which are rightmost in
\mathfrak{q}
.
Note that this notion depends on
\mathfrak{s}
only through its edge-set, so that
\mathfrak{s}
is rightmost if and only if any of the submaps of
\mathfrak{q}
which are re-rooting equivalent to
\mathfrak{s}
is rightmost.

Irreducible components

We are now equipped to define irreducible components, as well as component-rooted quadrangulations.

Definition 4.2
.

An
irreducible component
of a quadrangulation
\mathfrak{q}
is a rightmost submap
(\mathfrak{s},\alpha)
corresponding to an irreducible quadrangulation
\mathfrak{s}
.
It is automatically maximal with respect to the submap relation.
10
10

10

Indeed, if it is a submap of another irreducible quadrangulation
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
, then the borders of the faces of
\mathfrak{s}
cannot be separating in
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
, so that they also bound a face of
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
.

We recall that we made the convention that irreducible quadrangulations must have at least
4
faces.
In particular a quadrangulation must have at least
4
faces to possess an irreducible component.

Remark 4.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{q}
be a quadrangulation, and let
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
be the same quadrangulation obtained by re-rooting at some edge.
Let us justify that the collections of irreducible components of
\mathfrak{q}
and
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
coincide, when these components are viewed as
maps

\mathfrak{s}
, even though they may differ as
submaps

(\mathfrak{s},\alpha)
.

Indeed, either
\mathfrak{q}
and
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
have the same canonical bi-coloring, in which case the irreducible components of
\mathfrak{q}
and of
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
are identified; or the bi-coloring of
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
is obtained from that of
\mathfrak{q}
by switching colors.
In the latter case, this reverses the canonical orientation, so that irreducible components of
\mathfrak{q}
are identified with “leftmost” irreducible submaps of
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
instead.
These are in bijection with irreducible components of
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
by taking their “rightmost version”, that is by replacing each of their edges by the rightmost edge of
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
