with same endpoints.
Clearly, under this bijection, the irreducible
submaps
of
\mathfrak{q}
and
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
which are identified correspond to isomorphic
maps
.

The following lemma is quite intuitive and it will be essential in cases where we want to say that “there is a unique largest component”.

Lemma 4.4

(Separation lemma)
.

Let
\mathfrak{q}
be a quadrangulation and
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{s}_{2}
be two of its irreducible components.
If
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
is not
re-rooting equivalent
to
\mathfrak{s}_{2}
, then there exists a simple 4-cycle which separates
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{2}
, that is a simple 4-cycle
C
such that
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
is on one side of
C
and
\mathfrak{s}_{2}
on the other.

We will need the following intermediate lemma.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{q}
be a quadrangulation and let
\mathfrak{s}
be one of its irreducible components.
For every oriented rightmost simple 4-cycle
\vec{C}
on
\mathfrak{q}
, either
\mathfrak{s}
in in the interior of
\vec{C}
, or it is in its exterior.

Proof of Lemma
4.5
.

Suppose for the sake of contradiction that
\mathfrak{s}
intersects strictly both the interior of
\vec{C}
and its exterior.
It is well-known that irreducible quadrangulations are 3-connected, that is that one needs to remove at least 3 vertices to disconnect them.
In particular,
\mathfrak{s}
must contain at least three of the vertices on the cycle
\vec{C}
.
Let us therefore say that
\vec{C}
visits vertices
v_{1}
,
v_{2}
,
v_{3}
,
v_{4}
in that order, where
\mathfrak{s}
contains
v_{1}
,
v_{2}
,
v_{3}
, and possibly
v_{4}
.
We write
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
for the edges of
\vec{C}
joining
v_{1}
to
v_{2}
, resp.
v_{2}
to
v_{3}
.

We claim that
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
both belong to
E(\mathfrak{s})
.
Suppose that is not the case, say
e_{1}\notin E(\mathfrak{s})
.
Then,
e_{1}
is a path in the interior of some face
f
of
\mathfrak{s}
, between the vertices
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, which are on the boundary of
f
.
But
\mathfrak{q}
being bipartite implies that
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are of different colors, which implies that they are actually adjacent vertices on
\vec{\partial}f
since it is a 4-cycle.
All in all, we deduce that there is an edge
e^{\prime}_{1}\in E(\mathfrak{s})
between
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
.
Now, since
\vec{C}
is a rightmost cycle and
\mathfrak{s}
is a rightmost submap, we deduce that
e_{1}
and
e^{\prime}_{1}
are actually both the rightmost edge between
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
.
Hence
e_{1}\in E(\mathfrak{s})
, which gives a contradiction and proves the claim.

Now observe that
v_{4}
is a vertex of
\mathfrak{s}
.
Otherwise, the edges
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
, together with the boundary
\vec{\partial}f^{\prime}
of the face
f^{\prime}
containing
v_{4}
, would create a ‘‘4-cycle with a diagonal’’, which cannot happen in an irreducible quadrangulation.
11
11

11

This uses our convention that irreducible quadrangulations must have at least 4 faces.
Otherwise, the unique irreducible quadrangulation with 3 faces possesses a “4-cycle with a diagonal”.

Hence,
v_{4}
is a vertex of
\mathfrak{s}
, so that re-using the arguments at the beginning of this proof, the edges of
\vec{C}
actually all belong to
\mathfrak{s}
.
Since
\mathfrak{s}
intersects strictly both the interior of
\vec{C}
and its exterior, we obtain that
\vec{C}
is a separating 4-cycle in
\mathfrak{s}
, which contradicts the irreducibility of
\mathfrak{s}
.
This concludes our proof by contradiction.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{q}
be a quadrangulation, let
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{s}_{2}
be two of its irreducible components and suppose that
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{2}
are not
re-rooting equivalent
, that is
E(\mathfrak{s}_{1})\neq E(\mathfrak{s}_{2})
.
In particular, up to exchanging
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{2}
, there is some edge
e_{1}\in E(\mathfrak{s}_{1})\setminus E(\mathfrak{s}_{2})
.
Hence, under a proper embedding of
\mathfrak{q}
in the 2-sphere, the edge
e_{1}
, seen as a path on the sphere, must belong—except maybe at its endpoints—to the strict interior of some face
f_{2}
of
\mathfrak{s}_{2}
.
Since
\mathfrak{s}_{2}
is a rightmost and simple submap, the cycle
\vec{\partial}f_{2}
is simple and rightmost.
Hence it follows by Lemma
4.5
and the fact that
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
intersects the strict interior of
\vec{\partial}f_{2}
that it cannot also intersect its strict exterior.
But
\mathfrak{s}_{2}
is on the exterior of
\vec{\partial}f_{2}
since
f_{2}
is one of its faces.
Hence
\vec{\partial}f_{2}
separates
\mathfrak{s}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{2}
.
∎

Reconstruction

We will use the “detach” and “glue” operations from Section
4.1
.
Once and for all, we choose an algorithm
\psi
which to a quadrangulation
\mathfrak{q}
and a face
f
on it associates an oriented edge
\psi(\mathfrak{q};f)
on the oriented cycle
\vec{\partial}f
,
which we furthermore require to be white-to-black
.

For
4\leq\ell\leq n
, we let
{\mathcal{Q}}_{n}^{(\ell)}
denote the set of tuples
(\mathfrak{q};\mathfrak{s},\alpha)
where
\mathfrak{q}
is a quadrangulation with
n
faces, and
(\mathfrak{s},\alpha)
is an irreducible component of
\mathfrak{q}
with
\ell
faces.
We wish to describe the image of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{n}^{(\ell)}
under the mapping
\Psi
from Section
4.1
, with the above choice of algorithm
\psi
.

To this end, we let
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}
be the set of quadrangulations
\mathfrak{q}
such that both the root edge and its opposite edge in the root face are
solo edges
—we call
solo edge
an edge such that no other edge has the same endpoints.
Then, for
4\leq\ell\leq n
, we let
\mathcal{D}_{n,\ell}
be the set of tuples
(\mathfrak{q}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{q}_{\ell})
such that each
\mathfrak{q}_{i}
belongs to
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}
and such that
\sum_{i}|\mathfrak{q}_{i}|=n+\ell
.

Lemma 4.6
.

For
4\leq\ell\leq n
, the mapping
\Psi
induces a bijection

\displaystyle\Psi\colon{\mathcal{Q}}_{n}^{(\ell)}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{irr}}_{\ell}\times\mathcal{D}_{n,\ell}\times\{1,\dots,4n\},

whose inverse is given by
\mathrm{\Phi}
.

Proof.

First, it is justified in Section
4.1
that
\Psi
is bijective with the domain of definition and image that are given there.
Hence to prove the lemma, we only need to prove that the image of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{n}^{(\ell)}
under
\Psi
is the set
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{irr}}_{\ell}\times\mathcal{D}_{n,\ell}\times\{1,\dots,4n\}
.
Fix
4\leq\ell\leq n
and let
(\mathfrak{q},\mathfrak{s},\alpha)\in\smash{{\mathcal{Q}}_{n}^{(\ell)}}
.
We form
\Psi(\mathfrak{q};\mathfrak{s},\alpha)=(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{D}=(\mathfrak{q}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq\ell},\mathfrak{j})
.
By definition of irreducible components, we have
\mathfrak{s}\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{irr}}_{\ell}
.
We also need to justify that
\mathfrak{D}\in\mathcal{D}_{n,\ell}
.
By construction, we have
|\mathfrak{q}|=\sum_{1\leq i\leq\ell}(|\mathfrak{q}_{i}|-1)
, that is
\sum_{i}|\mathfrak{q}_{i}|=n+\ell
.
On the other hand, recall that irreducible components are required to be rightmost.
From the definition of rightmost submaps, it follows easily that, on the (counter-clockwise) oriented boundary
\vec{\partial}f
of a face
f
of
\mathfrak{s}
, the white-to-black edges are such that no other edge with same endpoints visits the interior of
f
—that is they induce
solo edges
in the map that is obtained from the “detaching operation” applied to the face
f
.
With the requirement we made that the rooting algorithm
\psi
chooses a white-to-right edge to root the
(\mathfrak{q}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq\ell}
, we therefore deduce that each
\mathfrak{q}_{i}
is in fact in
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}
.
We have therefore verified that
(\mathfrak{s},(\mathfrak{q}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq\ell},\mathfrak{j})
belongs to
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{irr}}_{\ell}\times\mathcal{D}_{n,\ell}\times\{1,\dots,4n\}
.
Similar considerations show that the elements of this set are sent into
\smash{{\mathcal{Q}}_{n}^{(\ell)}}
by the mapping
\mathrm{\Phi}
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

4.3.
Blocks and largest components

In
[
23
]
, Gao and Wormald studied several notions of “components”—which we shall call
blocks
here—and proved asymptotic results for the size of the largest such block in several models.
Roughly, in their approach, it is shown that a map
\mathfrak{m}
from some ensemble
\mathcal{A}
can be built from a “root-block”
\mathfrak{b}
belonging to some ensemble
\mathcal{B}
in which we have “substituted” maps from
\mathcal{A}
in place of the edges/faces/…, and where
\mathfrak{m}
inherits the root edge of
\mathfrak{b}
.
The blocks of
\mathfrak{m}
are then all the root-blocks that can be obtained upon starting with an other rooting of
\mathfrak{m}
.
Let us describe how one defines the
simple root-block of a general quadrangulation
, as well as the
irreducible root-block of a simple quadrangulation
.

Simple root-block

The following construction is dual, via Tutte’s “angular” bijection, to the decomposition of general maps as a collection of a map as a “2-connected root-block”, in the corners of which some maps are grafted.
See the original work of Tutte
[
49
]
for this decomposition, and
[
20
]
for the equivalence with the construction below.

Given a
general
quadrangulation
\mathfrak{q}
, its
simple root-block

\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(\mathfrak{q})
is obtained as follows: (i) embed
\mathfrak{q}
in the plane in such a way that the unbounded face lies to the right of the oriented root edge; and, (ii) for each maximal
12
12

12

A 2-cycle is
maximal
under this embedding in the plane if it is not contained in the bounded side of another 2-cycle.

2-cycle in
\mathfrak{q}
, empty its bounded side and collapse the 2-gonal face thus obtained into a single edge.
Note that root edge may have been “fused” with another edge in the process.

Irreducible root-block

We follow the approach of Mullin and Schellenberg
[
43
]
.
Given a
simple
quadrangulation
\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{s}}
, its
irreducible root-block

\mathfrak{B}{}^{\mathrm{irr}}(\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{s}})
is obtained as follows.
Embed
\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{s}}
in the plane in such a way that the unbounded face lies to the right of the oriented root edge, or equivalently such that the oriented boundary
\vec{\partial}f_{0}
of the root face
f_{0}
runs clockwise.
We then distinguish the two following cases:

(1)

If there is a “diagonal”, that is a path of length two in the strict interior of
\vec{\partial}f_{0}
joining two antipodal vertices of
\vec{\partial}f_{0}
, then
\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{s}}
will be said to have no root-block, which we write symbolically
\mathfrak{B}{}^{\mathrm{irr}}(\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{s}})=\varnothing
.

(2)

Otherwise, the irreducible root-block
\mathfrak{B}{}^{\mathrm{irr}}(\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{s}})
is obtained by emptying the bounded side of each maximal
13
13

13

A 4-cycle
C
is
maximal
if it is not the boundary of the root face and if it is not contained in the bounded side of another such 4-cycle.
In order to empty maximal 4-cycles, we need their bounded sizes to be non-intersecting.
This is the case when there is no “diagonal”, as the reader may verify, see
[
43
]
for an extensive discussion.

4-cycle of
\mathfrak{q}
.

Relation with Definition
4.2

The following lemma verifies that Definition
4.2
is consistent with the above notions of blocks.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{q}
be a quadrangulation.

(1)

For every irreducible component
(\mathfrak{s},\alpha)
of
\mathfrak{q}
, if we let
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
be
\mathfrak{q}
re-rooted at the oriented root edge of
\mathfrak{s}
, then we have
\mathfrak{s}=\mathfrak{B}{}^{\mathrm{irr}}(\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}))
.

(2)

For every
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
obtained by re-rooting
\mathfrak{q}
at some oriented edge, if
\mathfrak{B}{}^{\mathrm{irr}}(\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}))\neq\varnothing
, then
\mathfrak{B}{}^{\mathrm{irr}}(\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}))
can be realized as an irreducible component
(\mathfrak{s},\alpha)
of
\mathfrak{q}
.

Proof.

Let us begin with the first statement.
Let
\mathfrak{s}
be an irreducible component of
\mathfrak{q}
, and let
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
be
\mathfrak{q}
re-rooted at the oriented root edge of
\mathfrak{s}
.
Since
\mathfrak{s}
is simple, all the 2-cycles of
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
are inside some face of
\mathfrak{s}
, and each one can only be incident to at most one edge of
\mathfrak{s}
.
In particular, the operation of collapsing maximal 2-cycles in
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
does not change
\mathfrak{s}
as a map, but it realizes it as a submap of
\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(\mathfrak{q}^{\prime})
, where again
\mathfrak{s}
has the same root edge as
\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(\mathfrak{q}^{\prime})
.
Now,
\mathfrak{s}
being irreducible implies that its faces are bordered by maximal 4-cycles of
\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(\mathfrak{q}^{\prime})
; and, using the “separation” Lemma
4.4
, there can be no other separating 4-cycles.
14
14

14

Please note that any cycle is trivially rightmost in a simple quadrangulation, so that we can apply the separation lemma.

In particular, emptying the maximal 4-cycles of
\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(\mathfrak{q}^{\prime})
to get
\mathfrak{B}{}^{\mathrm{irr}}(\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}))
in fact yields
\mathfrak{s}
, as needed.

As regards the second statement, let
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
be obtained from
\mathfrak{q}
by re-rooting at some oriented edge, and suppose that
\mathfrak{B}{}^{\mathrm{irr}}(\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}))\neq\varnothing
.
Then
\mathfrak{B}{}^{\mathrm{irr}}(\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}))
can clearly be identified with a unique submap of
\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(\mathfrak{q}^{\prime})
.
On the other hand,
\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(\mathfrak{q}^{\prime})
can be realized in a non-unique way as a submap of
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
, just by choosing for each
e\in\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(\mathfrak{q}^{\prime})
an edge
\alpha(e)
among those of
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
with same endpoints which have been collapsed to form
e
.
All in all,
\mathfrak{B}{}^{\mathrm{irr}}(\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}))
can be realized as a submap of
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
, and we can assume that it is a rightmost one just by taking its “rightmost version” as in Remark
4.3
.
This means that
\mathfrak{B}{}^{\mathrm{irr}}(\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}))
can be realized as an irreducible component of
\mathfrak{q}
, as needed.
∎

In particular, if
\mathfrak{q}
is a quadrangulation, then its irreducible components (as defined in Definition
4.2
) are precisely the “irreducible root-blocks of simple root-blocks of re-rooted versions of
\mathfrak{q}
”, but with an explicit realization as submaps of
\mathfrak{q}
.

Largest blocks/components

The formulation via
root-blocks
is interesting in that it allows to write identities for related generating functions, which then allow to compute asymptotics for the size and number of largest components in random quadrangulations using analytic tools.
For the cases at hand, this has been performed by Gao and Wormald
[
23
]
; see also Banderier, Flajolet, Schaeffer and Soria
et. al.

[
9
]
for a generalization to many models, as well as precise local limit results for component sizes.

For
\mathfrak{q}
a quadrangulation, we define the sizes of its largest simple/irreducible components as follows:

\displaystyle\ell^{\mathrm{s}}(\mathfrak{q})=\max_{\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}}|\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(\mathfrak{q}^{\prime})|\qquad\text{and}\qquad\ell^{\mathrm{irr}}(\mathfrak{q})=\max_{\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}}|\mathfrak{B}{}^{\mathrm{irr}}(\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}))|,

where
\mathfrak{q}^{\prime}
ranges over re-rootings of
\mathfrak{q}
.
Note in particular that if
\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{s}}
is a simple quadrangulation, then
\ell^{\mathrm{irr}}(\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{s}})=\max_{(\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{s}})^{\prime}}|\mathfrak{B}{}^{\mathrm{irr}}((\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{s}})^{\prime})|
.
Also, by Lemma
4.7
, for every quadrangulation
\mathfrak{q}
we have the alternative definition:

\displaystyle\ell^{\mathrm{irr}}(\mathfrak{q})=\max\left\{|\mathfrak{s}|\colon\text{$\mathfrak{s}$ irred.\penalty\ component of $\mathfrak{q}$}\right\}.

The following follows
15
15

15

To be precise, using
[
20
, Prop. 2.11]
, the statement about
\ell^{\mathrm{s}}(Q_{n})
is equivalent to (1) in
[
23
, Thm. 2]
, which concerns 2-connected blocks of maps.
The statement about
\ell^{\mathrm{irr}}({Q}^{\mathrm{s}}_{n})
corresponds to (6) in the same theorem since, as the authors recall, 3-connected blocks of 2-connected maps are dual to irreducible blocks of simple quadrangulations (a word of caution: the authors call
simple quadrangulations
“quadrangulations”, and they call
irreducible quadrangulations
“simple quadrangulations”).

from Theorem 2 of
[
23
]
.

Proposition 4.8

(Gao and Wormald, 1999)
.

For
n\geq 2
, let
Q_{n}
(resp.
{Q}^{\mathrm{s}}_{n}
) be a uniformly random general (reps. simple) quadrangulation with
n
faces.
Then, for every
\varepsilon>0
, we have the asymptotics

\displaystyle\ell^{\mathrm{s}}(Q_{n})=\frac{n}{3}+o(n^{2/3+\varepsilon})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\ell^{\mathrm{irr}}({Q}^{\mathrm{s}}_{n})=\frac{n}{3}+o(n^{2/3+\varepsilon}),

in probability as
n\to\infty
.

Gao and Wormald
[
23
]
also prove in their Lemma 4 that the largest irreducible component in a uniform simple quadrangulation is
unique
with high probability.
Using our “separation lemma”, we can adapt their arguments to get the following.
For
n\geq 2
, we let
Q_{n}
(resp.
{Q}^{\mathrm{s}}_{n}
) be a uniformly random general (reps. simple) quadrangulation with
n
faces.

Lemma 4.9
.

With probability
1-o(1)
as
n\to\infty
, the maximum
\ell^{\mathrm{irr}}(Q_{n})=\max\left\{|\mathfrak{s}|\colon\text{$\mathfrak{s}$ irred.\penalty\ component of $\mathfrak{q}$}\right\}
is attained for a unique irreducible component up to re-rooting equivalence.
16
16

16

We recall that two submaps are
re-rooting equivalent
if they have the same edge-sets.

Furthermore, for every
\varepsilon>0
, the second largest irreducible component has
o(n^{2/3+\varepsilon})
faces in probability.
The same goes when
Q_{n}
is replaced by
{Q}^{\mathrm{s}}_{n}
in the preceding.

Proof.

We adapt the arguments of
[
23
, Lemma 4]
to the statement concerning
Q_{n}
—the one about
{Q}^{\mathrm{s}}_{n}
is dealt with similarly.
Since
|{\mathcal{Q}}_{n}|\sim c\rho^{n}n^{-5/2}
for some
c,\rho>0
(see
[
49
]
), it suffices to show that, if we denote by
A_{n}
the set of quadrangulations having at least two non-equivalent irreducible components with at least
n^{2/3+\varepsilon}
faces, then
|A_{n}|=o(\rho^{n}n^{-5/2})
.
Let
\mathfrak{q}\in A_{n}
.
Two non-equivalent irreducible components are separated by a 4-cycle using the “separation” Lemma
4.4
.
If we (i) distinguish such a 4-cycle between the two largest components, and (ii) choose an oriented root-edge on it, (iii) cut
\mathfrak{q}
along the 4-cycle; then we obtain two rooted quadrangulations
\mathfrak{q}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{2}
whose number of faces are at least
n^{2/3+\varepsilon}
and sum to
n+2
.
To recover
\mathfrak{q}
, one only needs to glue the root faces of
\mathfrak{q}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{2}
, and choose the root edge among the
4n
available oriented edges.
Using that
|Q_{k}|\sim c\rho^{k}k^{-5/2}
, the preceding gives:

|A_{n}|\leq\sum_{k=n^{2/3+\varepsilon}}^{n-n^{2/3+\varepsilon}}4n\cdot|{\mathcal{Q}}_{k}|\cdot|{\mathcal{Q}}_{n+2-k}|=O(1)n^{-3/2}\rho^{n}\int_{n^{2/3+\varepsilon}}^{\infty}\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}{t^{5/2}}\\
=O(n^{-3\varepsilon/2})\rho^{n}n^{-5/2}.

Hence
|A_{n}|=o(\rho^{n}n^{-5/2})
, which concludes the proof.
∎

We can now use Proposition
4.8
and Lemma
4.9
to deduce asymptotics for the largest irreducible component in uniform general quadrangulations.
For
n\geq 2
, we still let
Q_{n}
be a uniformly random general quadrangulation with
n
faces.

Corollary 4.10
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
, we have the asymptotics

\displaystyle\ell^{\mathrm{irr}}(Q_{n})=\frac{n}{9}+o(n^{2/3+\varepsilon}),

in probability as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

We let
Q^{\prime}_{n}
be
Q_{n}
re-rooted at a uniformly random oriented edge among those which realize the maximum
\ell^{\mathrm{s}}(\mathfrak{q})=\max_{\vec{e}}|\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(Q_{n})|
.

We claim that there is an event
\mathcal{E}_{n}
of probability
1-o(1)
such that conditionally on
\mathcal{E}_{n}
, the following holds: (i) the block
\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{\prime}_{n})
is uniformly random in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{s}}_{N_{n}}
conditionally on its size
N_{n}:=\ell^{\mathrm{s}}(Q_{n})
; and, (ii) with probability
1-o(1)
conditionally on
\mathcal{E}_{n}
, a largest irreducible block of
\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{\prime}_{n})
is a largest irreducible block of
Q_{n}
, that is
\ell^{\mathrm{irr}}(Q_{n})=\ell^{\mathrm{irr}}(\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{\prime}_{n}))
.

Given the claim, by (i) we have conditionally on
\mathcal{E}_{n}
and
N_{n}
that the simple quadrangulation
\mathfrak{B}^{\mathrm{s}}(Q^{\prime}_{n})
has the same distribution as
{Q}^{\mathrm{s}}_{N_{n}}
, where
({Q}^{\mathrm{s}}_{k},k\geq 2)
are uniformly random simple quadrangulations with
k
faces sampled independently from previously defined random variables.
Hence, using (ii) and Proposition
4.8
, conditionally on
\mathcal{E}_{n}
, we have
\ell^{\mathrm{irr}}(Q_{n})\smash{\overset{(\mathrm{d})}{=}}\ell^{\mathrm{irr}}({Q}^{\mathrm{s}}_{N_{n}})
, and for every
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\ell^{\mathrm{irr}}({Q}^{\mathrm{s}}_{N_{n}})=\frac{\ell^{\mathrm{s}}(Q_{n})}{3}+o(\ell^{\mathrm{s}}(Q_{n})^{2/3+\varepsilon})=\frac{n}{9}+o(n^{2/3+\varepsilon}),

in probability as
n\to\infty
.
This proves the corollary.

We now turn to our claim above.
The event
\mathcal{E}_{n}
we consider is “
Q_{n}
has a unique largest simple component up to re-rooting equivalence, and all the other ones have size at most
n^{2/3+0.1}
”, where we define a
simple component
to be a maximal rightmost simple quadrangular submap—there is a similar equivalence between simple blocks and simple components as the one for irreducible blocks/components in Lemma
4.7
.
We omit the proof that
\mathcal{E}_{n}
has probability
1-o(1)
, which can be proven along similar lines as Lemma
4.9
; and that the resulting largest simple component is a uniformly distributed quadrangulation conditionally on its size—this appears with different definitions of components/blocks in
[
6
]
and
[
20
]
, or one can adapt the proof given in Section
4.4
below that the largest irreducible component in
Q_{n}
is uniform conditionally on its size and on being the unique largest.

We now justify (ii) in the claim.
It suffices to show that conditionally on the event
\mathcal{E}_{n}
, with probability
1-o(1)
, any largest irreducible component of
Q_{n}
is included in “the” unique largest
simple
component of
Q_{n}
.
The event that
\ell^{\mathrm{irr}}(Q_{n})\geq n^{2/3+0.1}
has probability
1-o(1)
, even after conditioning on
\mathcal{E}_{n}
; so it suffices to show that conditionally on both events, the preceding statement regarding largest components holds.
Hence, we now reason conditionally on both events.

We let
\mathfrak{s}
be a largest irreducible component of
Q_{n}
.
In particular, it is a rightmost simple quadrangular submap, and thus contained in a maximal such submap,
i.e.
it is contained in a
simple component

\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
.
Since on
\mathcal{E}_{n}
, only
the
unique largest simple component can have size more than
n^{2/3+0.1}
, and since
\mathfrak{s}
has size
\ell^{\mathrm{irr}}(Q_{n})\geq n^{2/3+0.1}
, we deduce that the preceding simple component
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
is actually
the
unique largest simple component of
Q_{n}
.
∎

4.4.
Proof of Lemma
2.1

For
n\geq 2
, let
Q_{n}
be a uniformly random general quadrangulation with
n
faces.
We let
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{n}
denote the event that:

(1)

the maximum
\ell^{\mathrm{irr}}(Q_{n})=\max\left\{|\mathfrak{s}|\colon\text{$\mathfrak{s}$ irred.\penalty\ component of $\mathfrak{q}$}\right\}
is attained for a unique irreducible component up to re-rooting equivalence; and,

(2)

the value
K^{\prime}_{n}=\ell^{\mathrm{irr}}(Q_{n})-\lfloor n/9\rfloor
satisfies
|K^{\prime}_{n}|\leq n^{2/3+0.1}
.

By Corollary
4.10
and Lemma
4.9
,
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{n}
has probability
1-o(1)
as
n\to\infty
.
For
n\geq 2
, we let
(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n},\mathfrak{D}_{n},\mathfrak{j}_{n})
be a sample of the conditional distribution with respect to
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{n}
of the tuple
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{D},\mathfrak{j})=\Psi(Q_{n};\mathfrak{s}_{\mathrm{max}},\alpha)
, where
(\mathfrak{s}_{\mathrm{max}},\alpha)
is a uniformly chosen largest irreducible component of
Q_{n}
—on the event
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{n}
, this amounts to fixing uniformly at random an oriented root edge for the “unique irreducible component up to re-rooting equivalence”.
Lastly, we let
N_{n}=\ell^{\mathrm{irr}}(Q_{n})=|\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}|
, which we write as
N_{n}=\lfloor n/9\rfloor+K_{n}
(notice in particular that
K_{n}
has the law of
K^{\prime}_{n}
conditioned to
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{n}
).
Let us verify that
(N_{n},\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n},\mathfrak{D}_{n})
,
n\geq 2
, thus defined satisfy the conclusions of Lemma
2.1
.

Observe that, apart from the irreducible components which are re-rooting equivalent to
\mathfrak{s}_{\mathrm{max}}
, the other irreducible components of
Q_{n}
are irreducible components of some
\mathfrak{q}_{i}
using the “separation lemma” Lemma
4.4
.
Hence the event
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{n}\cap\{\ell^{\mathrm{irr}}(Q_{n})=\ell\}
is precisely the event that
|\mathfrak{s}_{\mathrm{max}}|=\ell
and that
\mathfrak{D}\in\mathcal{D}_{n,\ell}^{<\ell}
; where
\mathcal{D}_{n,\ell}^{<\ell}
corresponds to those collections
(\mathfrak{q}_{i})_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{n,\ell}
for which we have
\ell^{\mathrm{irr}}(\mathfrak{q}_{i})<\ell
for all
i
.
Using that
\Psi
induces a bijection
\Psi\colon{\mathcal{Q}}_{n}^{(\ell)}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{irr}}_{\ell}\times\mathcal{D}_{n,\ell}\times\{1,\dots,4n\}
by Lemma
4.6
, we therefore conclude that, for
\ell
in the support of
\lfloor n/9\rfloor+K_{n}
, conditionally given
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{n}\cap\{\ell^{\mathrm{irr}}(Q_{n})=\ell\}
, the tuple
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{D},\mathfrak{j})=\Psi(Q_{n};\mathfrak{s}_{\mathrm{max}},\alpha)
is uniform on

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{irr}}_{\ell}\times\mathcal{D}_{n,\ell}^{<\ell}\times\{1,\dots,4n\}.

By construction, this means that, conditionally on
N_{n}=\ell
, the pair
(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n},\mathfrak{D}_{n})
is uniformly distributed on
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{irr}}_{\ell}\times\mathcal{D}_{n,\ell}^{<\ell}
.

The later directly gives us (ii) of Lemma
2.1
, and also gives us (iv) since for every
4\leq\ell\leq n
, the set
\mathcal{D}_{n,\ell}^{<\ell}
, is invariant under permutation of coordinates.
Let us therefore verify that (i) and (iii) hold.
First, recall that
N_{n}=\lfloor n/9\rfloor+K_{n}
.
The bound
|K_{n}|\leq n^{2/3+0.1}
holds by definition of the event
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{n}
and the fact that
K_{n}
is distributed as
K^{\prime}_{n}
conditioned to
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{n}
, and
\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}
is uniformly distributed on
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{irr}}_{N_{n}}
conditionally on
N_{n}
by the preceding paragraph.
This gives us (iii).
Lastly, observe that by construction,
\mathrm{\Phi}(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n};\mathfrak{D}_{n},\mathfrak{j}_{n})
has the same distribution as
(Q_{n};\mathfrak{s}_{\mathrm{max}},\alpha)
conditioned to the event
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{n}
.
Taking the first coordinates, this means that
Q^{\prime}_{n}=\Gamma(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n};\mathfrak{D}_{n},\mathfrak{j}_{n})
is distributed as
Q_{n}
conditioned to the event
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{n}
.
Since
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{n}
has probability
1-o(1)
, we conclude that the distribution of
Q^{\prime}_{n}
is at vanishing total variation distance from
\mathrm{Unif}({\mathcal{Q}}_{n})
.
This gives (i) and finishes the proof of Lemma
2.1
.∎

Now that Lemma
2.1
has been proven, we shall adress the second key lemma used in the proof of Theorem
1.7
—laid down in Section
2
—namely Lemma
2.3
. This is the purpose of the next section.

5.
Step II: Bottlenecks and Hausdorff convergence

By the Theorem of Le Gall and Miermont, that we recalled as Theorem
1.6
, we know that uniformly random quadrangulations with a large number of faces converge to the Brownian sphere in the GHP sense, after a suitable normalization.
On the other hand, in Section
4
we have seen that the largest simple/irreducible component in uniformly random general/simple quadrangulations with a large number of faces gathers a proportion of the mass which is bounded away from
0
.
The purpose of this section is to lay down some general topological and measure-theoretic arguments which will allow to deduce that these largest components converge jointly to the (same) Brownian sphere as the quadrangulation they are obtained from.

5.1.
Bottlenecks and convergence of geodesic spaces

Let us begin by recalling what a
geodesic space
is.

Definition 5.1
.

A compact metric space
(X,d)
is said to be a
geodesic space
if for every
x,y\in X
, there exists an isometry
p\colon[0,d(x,y)]\to X
such that
p(0)=x
and
p(d(x,y))=y
.
Such an isometry is called a
geodesic path
between
x
and
y
.

It is well-known that an Hausdorff limit of compact geodesic spaces is still a geodesic space
[
14
, Thm. 7.5.1]
.

Let
(Z,\delta)
be a fixed compact metric space.
For
A\subset Z
and
\varepsilon>0
, we let
A^{\varepsilon}=\{x\in Z\colon\delta(x,A)<\varepsilon\}
be the
\varepsilon
-neighborhood of
A
in
(Z,\delta)
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
X
and
Y
be two compact subsets of
(Z,\delta)
.
If
(X\cup Y,\delta)
is a geodesic space, then
X^{\varepsilon}\cap Y^{\varepsilon}\subset(X\cap Y)^{2\varepsilon}
for every
\varepsilon>0
.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and let
z\in X^{\varepsilon}\cap Y^{\varepsilon}
.
By definition, there exist
x\in X
and
y\in Y
such that
\delta(x,z)<\varepsilon
and
\delta(x,z)<\varepsilon
.
In particular, we have
\delta(x,y)<2\varepsilon
by the triangle inequality.
Since
X\cup Y
is a geodesic space, there exists a geodesic path
p\colon[0,\delta(x,y)]\to X\cup Y
such that
p(0)=x
and
p(\delta(x,y))=y
, as in Figure
2
.
In particular, for every
t\in[0,\delta(x,y)]
, we have
\delta(p(t),\{x,y\})=\min(t,\delta(x,y)-t)<\varepsilon
, using that
\delta(x,y)<2\varepsilon
.
Hence,
\delta(z,p(t))<\varepsilon+\max(\delta(z,x),\delta(z,y))<2\varepsilon
.
This proves that
p
takes its values in in
{B}(z,2\varepsilon)
.
Therefore, in order to prove that
z\in(X\cap Y)^{2\varepsilon}
, it suffices to show that we can find
t\in[0,\delta(x,y)]
such that
p(t)\in X\cap Y
.
If that were not the case, then
[0,1]=p^{-1}(X)\sqcup p^{-1}(Y)
would be a partition into closed sets of the connected space
[0,1]
, hence a contradiction.
∎

Figure 2.
Setup of the proof of Lemma
5.2
.
The geodesic path
p\colon[0,\delta(x,y)]\to X\cup Y
such that
p(0)=x
and
p(\delta(x,y))=y
has been represented in red.

Lemma 5.3
.

Consider two converging sequences
X_{n}\to X_{\infty}
and
Y_{n}\to Y_{\infty}
in the
\delta
-Hausdorff metric.
Suppose that
X_{n}\cup Y_{n}
is a geodesic space for every
n\geq 1
.
Then
X_{n}\cap Y_{n}\to X_{\infty}\cap Y_{\infty}
in the
\delta
-Hausdorff metric.

Proof.

It is easily seen from the definition of Hausdorff convergence that
X_{n}\cup Y_{n}\to X_{\infty}\cup Y_{\infty}
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
.
Since
X_{n}\to X_{\infty}
and
Y_{n}\to Y_{\infty}
in the
\delta
-Hausdorff metric, for
n
large we have
X_{n}\cap Y_{n}\subset(X_{\infty})^{\varepsilon}\cap(Y_{\infty})^{\varepsilon}\subset(X_{\infty}\cap Y_{\infty})^{2\varepsilon}
, where the second inclusion comes from Lemma
5.2
, using that
X_{\infty}\cup Y_{\infty}
is a geodesic space, since it is an Hausdorff limit of the geodesic spaces
X_{n}\cup Y_{n}
,
n\geq 1
.
Similarly, we also deduce from the convergences
X_{n}\to X_{\infty}
and
Y_{n}\to Y_{\infty}
that for large
n
, we have
Z_{\infty}=X_{\infty}\cap Y_{\infty}\subset(X_{n})^{\varepsilon}\cap(Y_{n})^{\varepsilon}\subset(X_{n}\cap Y_{n})^{2\varepsilon}
, using Lemma
5.2
and that
X_{n}\cup Y_{n}
is a geodesic space.
Hence for large
n
, we have
X_{n}\cap Y_{n}\subset(Z_{\infty})^{2\varepsilon}
and
Z_{\infty}\subset(X_{n}\cap Y_{n})^{2\varepsilon}
, and the result follows.
∎

Definition 5.4
.

A metric space
(X,d)
is
2-connected
if
X\setminus\{x_{0}\}
is connected for every
x_{0}\in X
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Suppose that
X_{n}\cup Y_{n}\to Z_{\infty}
in the
\delta
-Hausdorff metric, where
X_{n},Y_{n}
are compact subsets of
(Z,\delta)
for each
n
.
Assume that
X_{n}\cup Y_{n}
is a geodesic space for every
n\geq 1
.
If
Z_{\infty}
is 2-connected and if
\mathrm{diam}(X_{n}\cap Y_{n})\to 0
, then
\min(\mathrm{diam}(X_{n}),\mathrm{diam}(Y_{n}))\to 0
.

Proof.

We only need
17
17

17

The general case follows, since given any subsequence of
(X_{n},Y_{n})_{n}
, we can extract further so that both coordinates converge in the Hausdorff sense, so that every subsequence of
(\min(\mathrm{diam}(X_{n}),\mathrm{diam}(Y_{n})))_{n}
has a subsequence converging to
0
, which gives that
\min(\mathrm{diam}(X_{n}),\mathrm{diam}(Y_{n}))\to 0
.
We used that the set of compact subsets of a compact metric space is itself compact when equipped with the Hausdorff metric.

to consider the case where
X_{n}\to X_{\infty}
and
Y_{n}\to Y_{\infty}
in the
\delta
-Hausdorff metric, for some compact subsets
X_{\infty},Y_{\infty}
of
Z_{\infty}
.
Then,
Z_{\infty}=X_{\infty}\cup Y_{\infty}
since both sides are the
\delta
-Hausdorff limits of
(X_{n}\cup Y_{n},n\geq 1)
.
We claim that
X_{\infty}\cap Y_{\infty}
is a singleton
\{z_{*}\}
for some
z_{*}\in Z_{\infty}
.
If this claim is true, then
Z_{\infty}\setminus\{z_{*}\}=(X_{\infty}\setminus\{z_{*}\})\sqcup(Y_{\infty}\setminus\{z_{*}\})
is a partition of
Z_{\infty}\setminus\{z_{*}\}
into two closed
18
18

18

The sets
(X_{\infty}\setminus\{z_{*}\})
and
(Y_{\infty}\setminus\{z_{*}\})
are closed in
Z_{\infty}\setminus\{z_{*}\}
, not in
Z_{\infty}
.

subsets, so that one of them must be empty since
Z_{\infty}\setminus\{z_{*}\}
is connected, which is absurd since
Z_{\infty}
is 2-connected.
Hence, either
X_{\infty}
is a singleton or
Y_{\infty}
is, so that the result follows by contradiction

We still have to prove our claim that
X_{\infty}\cap Y_{\infty}
is a singleton.
First, we have
X_{n}\cap Y_{n}\neq 0
for every
n\geq 1
, since otherwise we would have
Z_{n}=X_{n}\sqcup Y_{n}
, thus contradicting that
Z_{n}
is connected, as a geodesic space.
Hence taking a subsequential limit in a sequence of elements of
X_{n}\cap Y_{n}
respectively, we obtain an element
z_{*}\in X_{\infty}\cap Y_{\infty}
.
Now by Lemma
5.3
, we have
X_{n}\cap Y_{n}\to X_{\infty}\cap Y_{\infty}
in the
\delta
-Hausdorff metric, so that
\mathrm{diam}(X_{\infty}\cap Y_{\infty})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{diam}(X_{n}\cap Y_{n})=0
, and thus
X_{\infty}\cap Y_{\infty}
is actually reduced to the singleton
\{z_{*}\}
, as claimed.
∎

5.2.
Bottlenecks and “star-decompositions”

Let
(Z,\delta)
be a fixed compact metric space.
For
n\geq 1
, we let
Z_{n}
be a compact subspace of
(Z,\delta)
.
Suppose that for every
n\geq 1
, we are given a ‘‘star-decomposition’’
19
19

19

The name “star-decomposition” is motivated by the observation that when
Z_{n}
is connected, the incidence graph of the collection
(X_{n},Y^{1}_{n},Y^{2}_{n},\dots)
is a star-graph, where the center of the star is the vertex associated to
X_{n}
.

of
Z_{n}
as follows

\displaystyle Z_{n}=X_{n}\cup\bigsqcup_{i\geq 1}Y^{(i)}_{n},

where
X_{n}
is a non-empty compact subset of
Z_{n}
, and where
(Y^{(i)}_{n},i\geq 1)
is a collection of pairwise disjoint and possibly empty compact subsets of
Z_{n}
.
In this setting, we can refine Lemma
5.5
as follows.

Lemma 5.6
.

Suppose that
Z_{n}\to Z_{\infty}
in the
\delta
-Hausdorff metric, that each
Z_{n}
is geodesic, and that
Z_{\infty}
is 2-connected.
If it holds that

\displaystyle\textstyle\sup_{i\geq 0}\mathrm{diam}(X_{n}\cap Y^{(i)}_{n})\to 0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\textstyle\liminf_{n}\mathrm{diam}(X_{n})>0,

then
\sup_{i_{\geq}1}\mathrm{diam}(Y^{(i)}_{n})\to 0
, and thus
X_{n}\to Z_{\infty}
in the
\delta
-Hausdorff metric.

Proof.

It is easily verified from the definition of the Hausdorff metric that the convergence
\sup_{i_{\geq}1}\mathrm{diam}(Y^{(i)}_{n})\to 0
implies
\updelta_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{n},Z_{n})\to 0
and thus
X_{n}\to Z_{\infty}
in the
\delta
-Hausdorff metric, so let us prove that we indeed have
\sup_{i_{\geq}1}\mathrm{diam}(Y^{(i)}_{n})\to 0
.
Suppose for contradiction that
\sup_{i_{\geq}1}\mathrm{diam}(Y^{(i)}_{n})
does not go to
0
as
n\to\infty
.
Then, after possibly taking a subsequence, we may assume that there exists a sequence
(i_{n})_{n}
such that
\liminf_{n}\mathrm{diam}(Y^{(i_{n})}_{n})>0
.
We set for every
n\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\widehat{X}^{(i_{n})}_{n}=X_{n}\cup\bigsqcup_{i\neq i_{n}}Y^{(i)}_{n}.

Note that for every
n\geq 1
, we have
\widehat{X}^{(i_{n})}_{n}\cup Y^{(i_{n})}_{n}=Z_{n}
, which is a geodesic space, and the
\delta
-Hausdorff limit
Z_{\infty}
is 2-connected.
Also, since the
(Y^{(i)}_{n},i\geq 1)
are pairwise disjoint, we have
\widehat{X}^{(i_{n})}_{n}\cap Y^{(i_{n})}_{n}=X_{n}\cap Y^{(i_{n})}_{n}
, whose diameter goes to zero as
n\to\infty
by the assumption that
\sup_{i\geq 0}\mathrm{diam}(X_{n}\cap Y^{(i)}_{n})\to 0
.
Hence, we deduce from Lemma
5.5
that
\min(\mathrm{diam}(\widehat{X}^{(i_{n})}_{n}),\mathrm{diam}(Y^{(i_{n})}_{n}))\to 0
.
This gives the desired contradiction, since we have
\liminf_{n}\mathrm{diam}(Y^{(i_{n})}_{n})>0
by construction, and since we also have
\mathrm{diam}(\widehat{X}^{(i_{n})}_{n})\geq\mathrm{diam}(X_{n})
for every
n\geq 1
, where
\liminf_{n}\mathrm{diam}(X_{n})>0
by assumption.
∎

The condition that
\liminf_{n}\mathrm{diam}(X_{n})>0
in the preceding lemma may sometimes be difficult to establish.
Instead, it may be easier to prove that the “mass” of
X_{n}
does not vanish asymptotically.
We therefore adapt the preceding lemma as follows.

Again, for
n\geq 1
, we let
Z_{n}=X_{n}\cup\bigsqcup_{i\geq 1}Y^{(i)}_{n}
be a compact subspace of
(Z,\delta)
, where
X_{n}
is a non-empty compact subset of
Z_{n}
, and where
\smash{(Y^{(i)}_{n},i\geq 1)}
is a collection of pairwise disjoint and possibly empty compact subsets of
Z_{n}
.
On top of that, we let
\mu_{n}
be a finite Borel measure on
Z_{n}
for every
n\geq 1
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Suppose that
Z_{n}\to Z_{\infty}
in the
\delta
-Hausdorff metric, that each
Z_{n}
is geodesic, and that
Z_{\infty}
is 2-connected.
Suppose additionally that
\mu_{n}\to\mu_{\infty}
in the
\delta
-Prokhorov metric and that
\mu_{\infty}
is diffuse.
20
20

20

We recall that a Borel measure
\mu
is
diffuse
if every singleton has zero
\mu
-measure.

If it holds that

\displaystyle\textstyle\sup_{i\geq 0}\mathrm{diam}(X_{n}\cap Y^{(i)}_{n})\to 0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\textstyle\liminf_{n}\mu_{n}(X_{n})>0,

then
\sup_{i_{\geq}1}\mathrm{diam}(Y^{(i)}_{n})\to 0
, and thus
X_{n}\to Z_{\infty}
in the
\delta
-Hausdorff metric.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.6
, it is sufficient to prove that
\liminf_{n}\mathrm{diam}(X_{n})>0
.
Suppose for contradiction that this is not the case so that, for some subsequence
(X_{k_{n}})_{n}
, we have
\mathrm{diam}(X_{k_{n}})\to 0
.
By extracting further a subsequence, we can assume that
X_{k_{n}}\to X_{\infty}
in the
\delta
-Hausdorff metric, where
X_{\infty}
is necessarily a singleton
X_{\infty}=\{x_{\infty}\}
, since
\mathrm{diam}(X_{k_{n}})\to 0
.
Now, since
\mu_{n}\to\mu_{\infty}
in the
\delta
-Prokhorov metric, we have by the Portmanteau theorem that for all
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\mu_{\infty}\Bigl(\,\overline{{B}}^{(Z,\delta)}(x_{\infty},\varepsilon)\Bigr)\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mu_{k_{n}}\Bigl(\,\overline{{B}}^{(Z,\delta)}(x_{\infty},\varepsilon)\Bigr).

Since
X_{k_{n}}\to\{x_{\infty}\}
in the
\delta
-Hausdorff metric, we have
X_{k_{n}}\subset\overline{{B}}^{(Z,\delta)}(x_{\infty},\varepsilon)
for large
n
.
Hence, by the last display, we have

\displaystyle\mu_{\infty}\Bigl(\,\overline{{B}}^{(Z,\delta)}(x_{\infty},\varepsilon)\Bigr)\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mu_{k_{n}}(X_{k_{n}})\geq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}(X_{n}).

Hence, by taking
\varepsilon\to 0
and using the continuity of measure, we get that
\mu_{\infty}(\{x_{\infty}\})\geq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}(X_{n})
.
By assumption
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mu_{n}(X_{n})>0
, which gives is a contradiction since
\mu_{\infty}
is assumed to be diffuse.
∎

5.3.
Discrete approximations of the measures

We come back to the setting of the last section: we let
(Z,\delta)
be a compact space; for
n\geq 1
, we let
Z_{n}=X_{n}\cup\bigsqcup_{i\geq 1}\smash{Y^{(i)}_{n}}
be a compact subspace of
(Z,\delta)
, where
X_{n}
is a non-empty compact subset of
Z_{n}
, and where
\smash{(Y^{(i)}_{n},i\geq 1)}
is a collection of pairwise disjoint and possibly empty compact subsets of
Z_{n}
; and lastly, we let
\mu_{n}
be a finite Borel measure on
Z_{n}
for every
n\geq 1
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
5.7
, said lemma tells us that the
Y^{(i)}_{n}
,
n,i\geq 1
, have vanishing diameter as
n\to\infty
.
Therefore, it makes sense to approximate their contribution to the measure
\mu_{n}
by a discrete measure.
Suppose that we have fixed some
\smash{y_{n}^{(i)}\in Y^{(i)}_{n}}
for every
n,i\geq 1
.
Let us write
\gamma^{(i)}_{n}=\mu_{n}\bigl(Y^{(i)}_{n}\setminus X_{n}\bigr)
, and denote by
\mu^{X}_{n}=\mu_{n}(\cdot\cap X_{n})
the measure
\mu_{n}
restricted to
X_{n}
.
This allows to define for all
n\geq 1
the measure

(5.1)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)=\mu^{X}_{n}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)+\sum_{i\geq 1}\gamma^{(i)}_{n}\cdot\delta_{y^{(i)}_{n}}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x),

which will serve to approximate
\mu_{n}
.

Lemma 5.8
.

In the setting of Lemma
5.7
and with the preceding notation, we have
\updelta_{\mathrm{P}}(\widetilde{\mu}_{n},\mu_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, and in particular
\widetilde{\mu}_{n}\to\mu_{\infty}
in the
\delta
-Prokhorov metric.

Proof.

For
n,i\geq 1
, we let
\widetilde{Y}^{(i)}_{n}=Y^{(i)}_{n}\setminus X_{n}
, in order to lighten notation.
Let
\varepsilon>0
.
Fix
n
large enough such that
\sup_{i_{\geq}1}\mathrm{diam}(Y^{(i)}_{n})\leq\varepsilon
using Lemma
5.7
.
Now given
A\subset Z_{n}
a Borel subset, we have by
\sigma
-additivity,

\displaystyle\mu_{n}(A^{\varepsilon})=\mu^{X}_{n}(A^{\varepsilon})+\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{n}\bigl(A^{\varepsilon}\cap\widetilde{Y}^{(i)}_{n}\bigr)

\displaystyle\geq\mu^{X}_{n}(A)+\sum_{i\geq 1}\gamma^{(i)}_{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\{\widetilde{Y}^{(i)}_{n}\subset A^{\varepsilon}\}}

\displaystyle\geq\mu^{X}_{n}(A)+\sum_{i\geq 1}\gamma^{(i)}_{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\{y^{(i)}_{n}\in A\}}

(5.2)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mu}_{n}(A),

where the last inequality uses that
\widetilde{Y}^{(i)}_{n}
is included in the
\varepsilon
-neighborhood of
y^{(i)}_{n}
for every
i\geq 1
, since
\sup_{i_{\geq}1}\mathrm{diam}(Y^{(i)}_{n})\leq\varepsilon
.
On the other hand, we have by definition that
\gamma^{(i)}_{n}=\mu_{n}(\widetilde{Y}^{(i)}_{n})
, which yields:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}_{n}(A^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\mu^{X}_{n}(A^{\varepsilon})+\sum_{i\geq 1}\gamma^{(i)}_{n}{\mathbbm{1}}_{\{y^{(i)}_{n}\in A^{\varepsilon}\}}

\displaystyle\geq\mu^{X}_{n}(A)+\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{n}\bigl(A\cap\widetilde{Y}^{(i)}_{n}\bigr){\mathbbm{1}}_{\{y^{(i)}_{n}\in A^{\varepsilon}\}}.

Now for
i\geq 1
, we either have
A\cap\widetilde{Y}^{(i)}_{n}=\varnothing
so that the corresponding term in the last sum is zero, or
A\cap\widetilde{Y}^{(i)}_{n}\neq\varnothing
which implies
y^{(i)}_{n}\in A^{\varepsilon}
since
\sup_{i_{\geq}1}\mathrm{diam}(Y^{(i)}_{n})\leq\varepsilon
.
Hence the indicator in the last displayed sum is redundant, and the last display reduces to

(5.3)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}_{n}(A^{\varepsilon})\geq\mu^{X}_{n}(A)+\sum_{i\geq 1}\mu_{n}\bigl(A\cap\widetilde{Y}^{(i)}_{n}\bigr)=\mu_{n}(A).

The combination of (
5.3
) and (
5.3
) gives by definition of the Prokhorov distance that
\updelta_{\mathrm{P}}(\mu_{n},\widetilde{\mu}_{n})\leq\varepsilon
.
Since this holds for all large enough
n
, the result follows.
∎

Figure 3.
The “comb” counter-example for Remark
5.10
.
Fix any continuous function
f\colon[0,1]\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
.
We let
Z_{n}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be defined by
Z_{n}=X_{n}\cup\bigsqcup_{i}Y^{(i)}_{n}
, where
X_{n}=[0,1]\times\{0\}
is equipped with its Lebesgue measure, and for every
n
, the segment
Y^{(i)}_{n}=\{i/n\}\times[0,\frac{1}{n}f(i/n)]
is also equipped with its Lebesgue measure.
Then we replace the induced metric on
Z_{n}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
by its
length metric

d_{n}
induced by the length of shortest paths
inside

Z_{n}
, so as to turn
Z_{n}
into a geodesic space.
It is easily seen that
(Z_{n},d_{n},\mu_{n})
converges in the GHP metric to
[0,1]
with its usual distance, and equipped with the measure
(1+f(x))\mathrm{Leb}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)
.
Such a convergence can be made to hold in the Hausdorff+Prokhorov sense inside some compact space
(Z,\delta)
by Proposition
3.4

.

As a first step towards controlling the discrete part in (
5.1
), we prove the following lemma.

Lemma 5.9
.

In the setting of Lemma
5.7
, we have
\sup_{i_{\geq}1}\mu_{n}(Y^{(i)}_{n})\to 0
, and therefore also
\smash{\sup_{i\geq 1}\gamma^{(i)}_{n}\to 0}
, as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

If that is not the case, then using that
\sup_{i_{\geq}1}\mathrm{diam}(Y^{(i)}_{n})\to 0
by Lemma
5.7
, an argument along the same lines as the one given in the proof of this lemma shows that
\mu_{\infty}
is not diffuse, leading to a contradiction.
∎

Remark 5.10
.

Note that even when
\sup_{i_{\geq}1}\mu_{n}(Y^{(i)}_{n})\to 0
, there is still a lot of liberty in what can happen to the measures
\smash{\mu^{X}_{n}}
and in particular they can be very different from
\mu_{\infty}
as
n\to\infty
.
See for instance the “comb” example presented in Figure
3
, in which
X_{\infty}=[0,1]
, with
\mu_{\infty}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)=(1+f(x))\cdot\mathrm{Leb}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)
but
\mu^{X}_{n}\to\mathrm{Leb}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)
.

5.4.
Proof of Lemma
2.3

We place ourselves in the setting of Lemma
2.1
.
That is, we have random integers
(N_{n})_{n\geq 4}
, and random tuples

(5.4)

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n},\mathfrak{D}_{n},\mathfrak{j}_{n})\qquad\text{in}\qquad{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{irr}}_{N_{n}}\times\mathcal{D}_{n,N_{n}}\times\{1,\dots,4n\}

for
n\geq 4
, such that:
(i) the law of
Q^{\prime}_{n}:=\Gamma(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n};\mathfrak{D}_{n},\mathfrak{j}_{n})
is at
o(1)
total variation distance from
\mathrm{Unif}({\mathcal{Q}}_{n})
as
n\to\infty
; (ii)

\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}
is uniformly distributed in
\smash{{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{irr}}_{N_{n}}}
conditionally on
N_{n}
; (iii) we have the deterministic bound
|K_{n}|\leq n^{2/3+0.1}
where
K_{n}=N_{n}-\lfloor n/9\rfloor
; and, (iv) the collection
\mathfrak{D}_{n}=(\mathfrak{q}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{q}_{N_{n}})\in\mathcal{D}_{n,N_{n}}
is exchangeable conditionally on
N_{n}
.

Since the
Q^{\prime}_{n}
is at vanishing total variation distance from
\mathrm{Unif}({\mathcal{Q}}_{n})
when
n\to\infty
, the result of Le Gall
[
33
]
and Miermont
[
41
]
recalled as Theorem
1.6
still holds when replacing
Q_{n}
by
Q^{\prime}_{n}
in its statement.
In particular, using the Skorokhod representation theorem, we can assume that we have a coupling of the random quadrangulations
(Q^{\prime}_{n},n\geq 2)
and of an instance
(S,d,\mu)
of the Brownian sphere, such that the convergence

(5.5)

\displaystyle\left({V}(Q^{\prime}_{n}),\,\Bigl(\frac{9}{8n}\Bigr)^{1/4}\cdot d_{Q^{\prime}_{n}},\,\frac{1}{n+2}\cdot\mu_{Q^{\prime}_{n}}\right)\xrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{}(S,d,\mu)

holds in the almost sure sense with respect to the GHP metric.
If, for every
n
, we furthermore re-sample the random variables
(N_{n},\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n},\mathfrak{D}_{n})
conditionally given the coupling
(Q^{\prime}_{n},n\geq 2)
, then we can assume that all the random variables
((Q^{\prime}_{n},N_{n},\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n},\mathfrak{D}_{n}),n\geq 2)
are coupled in such a way that (i) the preceding almost sure convergence holds and (ii) the conclusions of Lemma
2.1
listed after (
5.4
) are satisfied.

We recall that the Brownian sphere
(S,d,\mu)
is almost surely homeomorphic to the two-dimensional sphere
[
35
,
39
]
, and in particular 2-connected.
Furthermore, its mass measure
\mu
is almost surely diffuse by
[
40
, Thm. 3]
.
We will therefore reason deterministically on the almost-sure event that (
5.5
) holds, that
Q^{\prime}_{n}=\Gamma(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n};\mathfrak{D}_{n},\mathfrak{j}_{n})
for every
n
, and that
(S,d,\mu)
is 2-connected with diffuse measure.

We recall that
\Gamma
corresponds to the first component of the mapping
\mathrm{\Phi}
, so let us write
\mathrm{\Phi}(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n};\mathfrak{D}_{n},\mathfrak{j}_{n})=(Q^{\prime}_{n},\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n},\alpha_{n})
.
In particular,
(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n},\alpha_{n})
is a submap of
Q^{\prime}_{n}
.
It is an easy exercise to verify that (
5.5
) also holds upon replacing its left-hand side by the
metric graph
of
Q^{\prime}_{n}
similarly renormalized, where this metric graph is obtained by realizing geometrically each edge using a unit segment with unit mass joining its endpoints.
Clearly, this turns the left-hand side of (
5.5
) into a geodesic space, and it converges in the GHP sense to
(S,d,\mu)
which is
2-connected
with
diffuse
measure.
Using (a GHP variant of) Gromov’s embedding theorem, for instance Proposition
3.4
, we can assume that the latter convergence holds in the Hausdorff–Prokhorov sense inside some compact metric space
(Z,\delta)
.

This is almost the setting of Lemma
5.7
.
We let
Z_{n}
be the metric graph of
Q^{\prime}_{n}
with distances renormalized as in the left-hand side of (
5.5
), which is therefore a geodesic compact subset of
(Z,\delta)
, and we let
\mu_{n}
be the image measure of
\frac{1}{n+2}\mu_{Q^{\prime}_{n}}
under the natural mapping
V(Q^{\prime}_{n})\to Z_{n}
.
Then, we let
X_{n}
be the subset of
Z_{n}
corresponding to
\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}
.
More precisely, recall that
(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n},\alpha_{n})
is a submap of
Q^{\prime}_{n}
, so that its metric graph naturally corresponds to a subset of the metric graph
Z_{n}
of
Q^{\prime}_{n}
.

We now make an innocent but crucially important observation:
X_{n}
equipped with the distance induced by that of
Z_{n}
is actually
isometric
to the metric graph of
\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}
with distances rescaled by
\bigl({9}/{8n}\bigr)^{1/4}
.
Equivalently, the graph distance on the vertices of
\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}
is precisely the distance induced on
V(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n})
by the graph distance of
Q^{\prime}_{n}
.
This follows from the observation that given a quadrangulation
\mathfrak{q}
and one of its irreducible components
\mathfrak{s}
, there is no strict shortcut between two points of
\mathfrak{s}
using edges of
E(\mathfrak{q})\setminus E(\mathfrak{s})
.
Indeed, the antipodal vertices on a 4-cycle cannot be at distance
0
, for otherwise the 4-cycle is not simple; neither at distance
1
, since a quadrangulation is bipartite.

Now, since
\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}
is a quadrangulation and a submap of
Q^{\prime}_{n}
, the connected components
(Y_{n}^{(i)})_{i}
of
Z_{n}\setminus X_{n}
each intersect
X_{n}
at points which correspond (under the metric graph construction) to vertices of
Q^{\prime}_{n}
on the boundary of a face of
\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}
, and said boundary is a 4-cycle.
Hence, in view of how distances are renormalized in the definition of
Z_{n}
, we deduce that
\sup_{i}\mathrm{diam}(X_{n}\cap Y_{n}^{(i)})\leq 4\cdot(9/8n)^{1/4}\to 0
.
Lastly, by construction
\mu_{n}(X_{n})
equals
(N_{n}+2)/(n+2)
, where
N_{n}+2
is the number of vertices of
\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}
.
Under our coupling, we have
\liminf_{n}\mu_{n}(X_{n})=\liminf N_{n}/n=1/9
almost surely, which is positive.

All in all, the assumptions of Lemma
5.7
have all been checked, allowing us to deduce that
(X_{n})_{n}
converges almost surely to the Brownian sphere
Z_{\infty}=\lim_{n}Z_{n}
in the Hausdorff sense.
Also, using Lemma
5.8
, we deduce that the image of the measure
\frac{1}{n+2}\cdot(\mu_{\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}}+\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}})
(defined in the statement of Lemma
2.3
), under the metric graph construction and the embedding in
(Z,\delta)
, converges as
n\to\infty
to the measure
\mu_{\infty}=\lim_{n}\mu_{n}
carried by the Brownian sphere
Z_{\infty}
, almost surely in the Prokhorov sense.
Also, by Lemma
5.9
, we have that
\sup_{x}\frac{1}{n+2}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}}(x)\to 0
almost surely.

We can now conclude.
By the preceding, abstracting away from the embedding into
(Z,\delta)
, we get that the metric graph version of
\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}
(with distances renormalized by
(9/8n)^{1/4}
), equipped with the measure
\frac{1}{n+2}\cdot(\mu_{\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}}+\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}})
, converges almost surely to
(S,d,\mu)
in the above coupling.
Again, one easily switches between the metric graph version and the vertex set equipped with the graph distance, which gives us the convergence

\displaystyle\left({V}(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}),\,\left(\frac{9}{8n}\right)^{1/4}\cdot d_{\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}},\,\frac{1}{n+2}\cdot(\mu_{\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}}+\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}})\right)\xrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{(\mathrm{d}),\,\mathrm{GHP}}(S,d,\mu),

as needed.
To complete the proof of Lemma
2.3
, note that we have already justified that
\sup_{x}\frac{1}{n+2}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}}(x)\to 0
almost surely in the above coupling, and note also that the exchangeability statement about the measures
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}}
,
n\geq 2
, follows from the exchangeability of each
\mathfrak{D}_{n}
conditionally on
N_{n}
, as given by Lemma
2.1
.
Hence the proof of Lemma
2.3
is complete.∎

In the next section, we continue our proof of the key lemmas presented in Section
2
and establish Lemma
2.5
.

6.
Step III: Exchangeability and Prokhorov convergence

This section is a continuation of Section
5
.
Our aim is now to gain control on the measure part in Lemma
2.3
, so as to replace
\frac{1}{n+2}\cdot(\mu_{\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}}+\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}})
by a constant multiple of
\frac{1}{n+2}\cdot\mu_{\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}}
.
Unfortunately, deterministic topological/measure-theoretic arguments are not sufficient in general for that purpose, see Remark
5.10
.
Using ideas of Addario-Berry and Wen
[
6
]
, it is possible to circumvent this difficulty using the
exchangeability
property that our random measure
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}}
satisfies.
We revisit their argument in a general context, and propose a replacement for a concentration bound that seems to contain an error, see Remark 6.4. This is then applied to prove Lemma
2.5
.

6.1.
Revisiting the Addario-Berry–Wen exchangeability argument

A random collection indexed by a finite set is said to be
exchangeable
if its distribution is invariant under permutation of the coordinates. For instance, the random vector
(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})
is exchangeable if for every permutation
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, the vector
(\xi_{\sigma(1)},\dots,\xi_{\sigma(n)})
has the same distribution as
(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})
.

We let
(Z,\delta)
be a compact space, and we consider a sequence
(D_{n},n\geq 1)
of finite subsets of
Z
.
Suppose that
(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n},n\geq 1)
are
random
measures supported on
D_{n}
respectively.
We shall prove the following.

Proposition 6.1
.

Assume that the collection
(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}(x))_{x\in D_{n}}
is exchangeable
for every
n\geq 1
.
If
\max_{x\in D_{n}}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}(x)\to 0
in probability, then

\displaystyle\updelta_{\mathrm{P}}\Bigl(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n},|\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}|\cdot\mathrm{Unif}_{D_{n}}\Bigr)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0

in probability.

The remainder of this section is dedicated to the proof of Proposition
6.1
.
We first need the following lemma, which tells that the Prokhorov distance between two measures can be bounded by the total variation distance between suitable “coarse-grained” versions of the measures.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
, and consider a finite partition
Z=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{K_{\varepsilon}}Z_{i,\varepsilon}
into Borel subsets of the compact space
Z
, such that
\max_{i}\mathrm{diam}(Z_{i,\varepsilon})<\varepsilon
.
Then for any two finite Borel measures
\mu,\nu
on
Z
, we have:

(6.1)

\displaystyle\updelta_{\mathrm{P}}(\mu,\nu)\leq\varepsilon+\sum_{1\leq i\leq K_{\varepsilon}}|\mu(Z_{i,\varepsilon})-\nu(Z_{i,\varepsilon})|.

Proof.

For all Borel subset
A\subset Z
, using that the
(Z_{i,\varepsilon})_{i}
partition
Z
and that
\max_{i}\mathrm{diam}(Z_{i,\varepsilon})<\varepsilon
, we have:

\displaystyle\mu(A^{\varepsilon})=\sum_{i}\mu(A^{\varepsilon}\cap Z_{i,\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i}\mu(Z_{i,\varepsilon}){\mathbbm{1}}_{\{Z_{i,\varepsilon}\subset A^{\varepsilon}\}}\geq\sum_{i}\mu(Z_{i,\varepsilon}){\mathbbm{1}}_{\{A\cap Z_{i,\varepsilon}\neq\varnothing\}}.

If we let
\Delta=\sum_{i}|\mu(Z_{i,\varepsilon})-\nu(Z_{i,\varepsilon})|
, then we can lower bound further by:

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i}\nu(Z_{i,\varepsilon}){\mathbbm{1}}_{\{A\cap Z_{i,\varepsilon}\neq\varnothing\}}\right)-\Delta

\displaystyle\geq\left(\sum_{i}\nu(A\cap Z_{i,\varepsilon}){\mathbbm{1}}_{\{A\cap Z_{i,\varepsilon}\neq\varnothing\}}\right)-\Delta

\displaystyle=\nu(A)-\Delta.

Hence,
\mu(A^{\varepsilon})\geq\nu(A)-\Delta
, and also
\nu(A^{\varepsilon})\geq\mu(A)-\Delta
by symmetry.
Since this holds for all
A
, we get
\updelta_{\mathrm{P}}(\mu,\nu)\leq\varepsilon+\Delta
.
∎

Next, we will need the following concentration result which is proven in Section
6.2
.
It is used as a replacement for Addario-Berry and Wen’s Lemma 5.3 in
[
6
]
, see Remark
6.4
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
X
be a non-empty finite set and let
\boldsymbol{\nu}
be a random probability measure on
X
such that the collection
(\boldsymbol{\nu}(x))_{x\in X}
is exchangeable.
Then the inequality

\displaystyle\mathbf{Var}\Bigl(\boldsymbol{\nu}(A)\Bigr)\leq\frac{|A|}{|X|}\cdot\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,\max_{x\in X}\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\,}}\right]

holds for all
A\subset X
.

We can now prove Proposition
6.1
.

Proof of Proposition
6.1
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
.
Since
Z
is a compact space, one can easily construct a finite partition
Z=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{K_{\varepsilon}}Z_{i,\varepsilon}
into Borel subsets, in such a way that
\max_{i}\mathrm{diam}(Z_{i,\varepsilon})<\varepsilon
.
Fix
n\geq 1
.
By Lemma
6.2
we have:

\displaystyle\updelta_{\mathrm{P}}\Bigl(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n},|\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}|\cdot\mathrm{Unif}_{D_{n}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon+\sum_{1\leq i\leq K_{\varepsilon}}\Bigl|\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}(Z_{i,\varepsilon})-|\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}|\cdot\mathrm{Unif}_{D_{n}}(Z_{i,\varepsilon})\Bigr|

\displaystyle=\varepsilon+\sum_{1\leq i\leq K_{\varepsilon}}\Bigl|\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}(Z_{i,\varepsilon})-\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}(Z_{i,\varepsilon})\,}}\right]\Bigr|,

The last equality can be justified as follows: from the exchangeability of the collection
(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}(x))_{x\in D_{n}}
, we have

\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,|\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}|\,}}\right]=\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,\sum_{x\in D_{n}}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}(x)\,}}\right]=|D_{n}|\cdot\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,\boldsymbol{\nu}(x_{0})\,}}\right]

for every
x_{0}\in D_{n}
, and therefore
\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}(Z_{i,\varepsilon})\,}}\right]=\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,\sum_{x\in Z_{i,\varepsilon}}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}(x)\,}}\right]=|Z_{i,\varepsilon}|\cdot|\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}|/|D_{n}|
, that is
|\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}|\cdot\mathrm{Unif}_{D_{n}}(Z_{i,\varepsilon})
.
In particular, from the last display and Chebychev’s inequality we deduce the bound:

\P\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\,\updelta_{\mathrm{P}}\Bigl(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n},|\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}|\cdot\mathrm{Unif}_{D_{n}}\Bigr)\geq 2\varepsilon\,}}\right)\\
\begin{aligned} &\leq\P\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\,\sum_{1\leq i\leq K_{\varepsilon}}\Bigl|\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}(Z_{i,\varepsilon})-\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}(Z_{i,\varepsilon})\,}}\right]\Bigr|\geq\varepsilon\,}}\right)\\
&\leq\sum_{1\leq i\leq K_{\varepsilon}}\P\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\,\Bigl|\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}(Z_{i,\varepsilon})-\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}(Z_{i,\varepsilon})\,}}\right]\Bigr|\geq\varepsilon/K_{\varepsilon}\,}}\right)\\
&\leq\sum_{1\leq i\leq K_{\varepsilon}}(\varepsilon/K_{\varepsilon})^{-2}\cdot\mathbf{Var}\Bigl(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}(Z_{i,\varepsilon})\Bigr).\end{aligned}

Hence, using Lemma
6.3
to bound the above variances, we finally obtain:

\displaystyle\P\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\,\updelta_{\mathrm{P}}\Bigl(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n},|\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}|\cdot\mathrm{Unif}_{D_{n}}\Bigr)\geq 2\varepsilon\,}}\right)\leq K_{\varepsilon}\cdot(\varepsilon/K_{\varepsilon})^{-2}\cdot\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,\max_{x\in D_{n}}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}(x)\,}}\right].

We have by assumption
\max_{x\in D_{n}}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}(x)\to 0
in probability.
We also trivially have
\max_{x\in D_{n}}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}(x)\leq 1
since
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}
is a probability measure, so that the convergence
\max_{x\in D_{n}}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{n}(x)\to 0
holds in the
L^{1}
sense.
Hence, the right-hand side in the last display goes to zero as
n\to\infty
and the result follows.
∎

Remark 6.4
.

Instead of the variance estimate in Lemma
6.3
, Addario-Berry and Wen use the concentration bound
[
6
, Lemma 5.3]
, which seems to contain an error.
In substance, the claimed concentration bound amounts to saying
21
21

21

The bound (
6.2
) is a re-writing (with different notation) of the bound at the end of
[
6
, p. 1908]
.

that if we are given an exchangeable non-negative random vector
\boldsymbol{X}=(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
with
|\boldsymbol{X}|_{2}>0
, then for all
1\leq k\leq n
and all
t>0
we have

(6.2)

\displaystyle\P\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\,\Bigl|\sum_{i=1}^{k}(X_{i}-\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,X_{i}\,}}\right])\Bigr|>2t\;\middle|\;|\boldsymbol{X}|_{2}\,}}\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{2t^{2}}{|\boldsymbol{X}|_{2}^{2}}\right).

As a counter-example to this inequality, let
Y
be an arbitrary non-constant random variable on the positive reals.
For
n\geq 1
, if we consider the exchangeable vector
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=(Y,Y,\dots,Y)
, then
|\boldsymbol{X}|_{2}=\sqrt{n}Y
.
Hence the left-hand side in the last display is the indicator of the event
\{k\cdot|Y-\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,Y\,}}\right]|>2t\}
, while the right-hand side equals
2\exp(-2t^{2}/(nY^{2}))
.
For every
\varepsilon>0
and
n\geq 1
, upon setting
k=n
and
t=\varepsilon n
in (
6.2
) we would thus get:

\displaystyle{\mathbbm{1}}_{\{|Y-\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,Y\,}}\right]|>2\varepsilon\}}\leq 2\exp\left(-{2n\varepsilon^{2}}/{Y^{2}}\right).

The latter goes to zero as
n\to\infty
so that
Y
is almost surely equal to its mean, hence a contradiction.

6.2.
A variance bound for exchangeable random vectors

For
n\geq 1
and
{\mathbf{y}}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, we let:

\displaystyle|{\mathbf{y}}|_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i},\qquad|{\mathbf{y}}|_{2}=\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2},\qquad|{\mathbf{y}}|_{\infty}=\max_{i=1,\dots,n}y_{i}.

Our goal in this section is to prove Lemma
6.3
.
We will prove in fact the following more general statement.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
n\geq 1
, and let
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\dots,\xi_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be an exchangeable random vector such that
\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|_{2}^{2}\,}}\right]<\infty
.
We let
S_{k}=\xi_{1}+\dots+\xi_{k}
for
1\leq k\leq n
.
Then for all
1\leq k\leq n
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Var}(S_{k})\leq\frac{k}{n}\cdot\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|_{2}^{2}\Bigm.\!\,}}\right]+\frac{k^{2}}{n^{2}}\cdot\mathbf{Var}(S_{n}).

Before turning to the proof of Proposition
6.5
, let us show that Lemma
6.3
indeed follows from it.

Proof of Lemma
6.3
.

Let
A\subseteq X
, say
A\neq\varnothing
to avoid trivialities, and set
k=|A|
and
n=|X|
.
We enumerate the vertices of
X
as
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
, in such a way that
A=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\}
.
If we set
\xi_{\ell}=\boldsymbol{\nu}(x_{\ell})
for
1\leq\ell\leq n
, then by construction the vector
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})
is exchangeable and
\boldsymbol{\nu}(\{x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell}\})
equals
S_{\ell}=\xi_{1}+\dots+\xi_{\ell}
for every
1\leq\ell\leq n
.
Hence, by Proposition
6.5
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Var}(\boldsymbol{\nu}(A))=\mathbf{Var}(S_{k})\leq\frac{k}{n}\cdot\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|_{2}^{2}\Bigm.\!\,}}\right]+\mathbf{Var}(S_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{k}{n}\cdot\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|_{\infty}\Bigm.\!\,}}\right]+0

\displaystyle=\frac{|A|}{|X|}\cdot\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,\max_{x\in X}\boldsymbol{\nu}(x)\,}}\right],

where we used that
\boldsymbol{\nu}
is a probability measure, so that on the one hand
|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|_{2}^{2}\leq|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|_{1}\cdot|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|_{\infty}=1\cdot|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|_{\infty}
, and on the other hand
\mathbf{Var}(S_{n})=0
since
S_{n}=1
almost surely.
Since
A
is an arbitrary subset of
X
, the result follows.
∎

The remainder of Section
6.2
is dedicated to the proof of Proposition
6.5
.

We recall that given
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\P)
a probability space and
\mathcal{G}\subset\mathcal{F}
a
\sigma
-field, the conditional covariance of two random variables
X,Y\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\P)
is the random variable
\mathbf{Cov}(X,Y|\mathcal{G})\in L^{1}(\Omega,\mathcal{G},\P)
given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{Cov}(X,Y|\mathcal{G})

\displaystyle=\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,\bigl(X-\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,X\;\middle|\;\mathcal{G}\,}}\right]\bigr)\cdot\bigl(Y-\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,Y\;\middle|\;\mathcal{G}\,}}\right]\bigr)\Big.\!\;\middle|\;\mathcal{G}\,}}\right]

\displaystyle=\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,X\cdot Y\;\middle|\;\mathcal{G}\,}}\right]-\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,X\;\middle|\;\mathcal{G}\,}}\right]\cdot\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,Y\;\middle|\;\mathcal{G}\,}}\right].

The conditional covariance is linked with the unconditional covariance
via
:

(6.3)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Cov}(X,Y)=\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,\mathbf{Cov}(X,Y|\mathcal{G})\Bigm.\!\,}}\right]+\mathbf{Cov}\Bigl(\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,X\;\middle|\;\mathcal{G}\,}}\right],\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,Y\;\middle|\;\mathcal{G}\,}}\right]\Bigr).

In the following lemma, we make the simple observation that when conditioning with respect to the “empirical distribution” of an exchangeable vector
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
, conditional expectations can be expressed as “empirical expectations”.
More precisely, given
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, we denote by
\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
the class of
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
modulo the action of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
by permutation of coordinates.
Note that for
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, the class
\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
depends only on the empirical distribution
\sum_{i}\delta_{\xi_{i}}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y)
of
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
.
Then we have the following.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
n\geq 1
, and let
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\dots,\xi_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be an exchangeable random vector.
Then, for any measurable functions
f\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and
g\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that
f(\xi_{1})
and
g(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
are integrable, we have:

\displaystyle\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,f(\xi_{k})\;\middle|\;\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\,}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}f(\xi_{i}),

\displaystyle k

\displaystyle\in\{1,\dots,n\}

\displaystyle\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,g(\xi_{k},\xi_{\ell})\;\middle|\;\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\,}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}g(\xi_{i},\xi_{j}),

\displaystyle k,\ell

\displaystyle\in\{1,\dots,n\},\quad k\neq\ell.

Proof.

We fix
k,\ell\in\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
k\neq\ell
.
By definition of
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\mapsto\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
, conditionally on
\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
, the vector
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
is still exchangeable.
Hence, conditionally on
\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
, the pair
(\xi_{k},\xi_{\ell})
has the same distribution as
(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})
for every pair
(i,j)\in\{1,\dots,n\}^{2}
with
i\neq j
, so that:

\displaystyle\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,g(\xi_{k},\xi_{\ell})\Bigm.\!\;\middle|\;\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\,}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,g(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\Bigm.\!\;\middle|\;\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\,}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,\sum_{i\neq j}g(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})\;\middle|\;\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\,}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}g(\xi_{i},\xi_{j}),

where the last equality uses that
\sum_{i\neq j}g(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})
is a
\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
-measurable random variable since it is defined as a permutation-invariant function of
(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})
.
Similarly, conditionally on
\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
, the random variable
\xi_{k}
has the same distribution as
\xi_{i}
for every
1\leq i\leq n
, so that:

\displaystyle\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,f(\xi_{k})\;\middle|\;\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\,}}\right]=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,f(\xi_{i})\;\middle|\;\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\,}}\right]=\frac{1}{n}\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,\sum_{i}f(\xi_{i})\;\middle|\;\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\,}}\right]=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}f(\xi_{i}),

which concludes the proof.
∎

In particular, we obtain as an easy corollary the following negative dependence statement.

Corollary 6.7
.

Let
n\geq 1
, and let
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\dots,\xi_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be an exchangeable random vector such that
\xi_{1}
possesses a second moment.
Then, we have almost surely:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Cov}\Bigl(\xi_{k},\xi_{\ell}\bigm|\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\Bigr)\leq 0,

\displaystyle k,\ell\in\{1,\dots,n\},\quad k\neq\ell.

Proof.

We fix
k,\ell\in\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
k\neq\ell
.
By Lemma
6.6
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Cov}\bigl(\xi_{k},\xi_{\ell}\bigm|\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,\xi_{k}\cdot\xi_{\ell}\;\middle|\;\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\,}}\right]-\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,\xi_{k}\;\middle|\;\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\,}}\right]\cdot\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,\xi_{\ell}\;\middle|\;\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\,}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i,j}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{n(n-1)}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)\sum_{i,j}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}-\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i}\xi_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{n(n-1)}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right)n\sum_{i}\xi_{i}^{2}-\frac{1}{n(n-1)}\sum_{i}\xi_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=0,

using that
\sum_{i}\xi_{i}\leq\sqrt{n}\sqrt{\sum_{i}\xi_{i}^{2}}
by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality.
∎

Remark 6.8
.

Let us consider the case where
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\dots,\xi_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is an exchangeable random vector taking only a discrete set of values.
Then for
{\mathbf{y}}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
in the support of its distribution, conditionally on
\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}=\underline{{\mathbf{y}}}
, the vector
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
has the same distribution as
(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})=(y_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(1)},\dots,y_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(n)})
, where
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
is uniformly sampled in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
.
A distribution of this form is known to be
negatively associated
, see Joag-Dev and Proschan’s celebrated work
[
31
]
, and more precisely their Theorem 2.11.

Negative association
is a strong “negative dependence” property.
For the vector
(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})
, it means that for every partition
\{1,\dots,n\}=I\sqcup J
of the index set and for every coordinate-wise increasing functions
f\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{I}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and
g\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{J}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, we have
\mathbf{Cov}(f(Y_{i},i\in I),g(Y_{j},j\in J))\leq 0
.

We are now in a position to prove the main statement of this section.

Proof of Proposition
6.5
.

Let
1\leq k\leq n
.
By (
6.3
), we have:

(6.4)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Var}(S_{k})=\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,\mathbf{Var}\bigl(S_{k}\bigm|\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\bigr)\,}}\right]+\mathbf{Var}\Bigl(\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,S_{k}\;\middle|\;\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\,}}\right]\Bigr).

Let us begin by treating the first term.
By Corollary
6.7
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{Var}\bigl(S_{k}\bigm|\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{Var}\bigl(\xi_{i}\bigm|\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\bigr)+\sum_{i,j\leq k\,i\neq j}\mathbf{Cov}\bigl(\xi_{i},\xi_{j}\bigm|\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbf{Var}\bigl(\xi_{i}\bigm|\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,\xi_{i}^{2}\;\middle|\;\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\,}}\right].

Using Lemma
6.6
, this gives that:

(6.5)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Var}\bigl(S_{k}\bigm|\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\bigr)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}\xi_{i}^{2}\right)=\frac{k}{n}\cdot|{\boldsymbol{\xi}}|_{2}^{2}.

On the other hand, by Lemma
6.6
, we also have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Var}\Bigl(\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,S_{k}\;\middle|\;\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\,}}\right]\Bigr)=\mathbf{Var}\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\E\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\,\xi_{i}\;\middle|\;\underline{{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\,}}\right]\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Var}\Bigl(k\cdot\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}\Bigr)

(6.6)

\displaystyle=\frac{k^{2}}{n^{2}}\cdot\mathbf{Var}(S_{n}).

The result follows by combining (
6.4
) with (
6.5
) and (
6.2
). ∎

6.3.
Proof of Lemma
2.5

We place ourselves in the setting of Lemma
2.3
.
In particular, with the notation used there, we recall that
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)=\sum_{j}\bigl(|V(\mathfrak{q}_{j})|-4\bigr)\cdot\delta_{v_{j}}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x)
,
where
v_{j}
is a uniformly chosen vertex incident to the
j
-th face of
\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}
in
<_{\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}}
-order.
The former lemma gives that

(6.7)

\displaystyle\left({V}(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}),\,\left(\frac{9}{8n}\right)^{1/4}\cdot d_{\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}},\,\frac{1}{n+2}\cdot(\mu_{\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}}+\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}})\right)\xrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{(\mathrm{d}),\,\mathrm{GHP}}(S,d,\mu),

and that the collection
(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}}(v_{j}),1\leq j\leq N_{n})
is exchangeable conditionally on
N_{n}
and its supremum converges to
0
in probability as
n\to\infty
.
By the Skorokhod representation theorem, both the convergence (
6.7
) and the convergence
\to 0
can be strengthened to hold in the almost sure sense, up to properly coupling the involved random variables.

From now on, we reason conditionally on the sequences
(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n},n\geq 2)
and
(\sup_{x}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}}(x),n\geq 2)
, coupled as described precedingly.
Using an embedding theorem akin to Proposition
3.4
, we can assume that
({V}(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}),\,\left(\frac{9}{8n}\right)^{1/4}\cdot d_{\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}})
converges to
(S,d)
, in the Hausdorff distance inside some compact space
(Z,\delta)
.
Crucially, this compact
(Z,\delta)
, and the embedding of the
({V}(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}),\,\left(\frac{9}{8n}\right)^{1/4}\cdot d_{\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}})
,
n\geq 2
into it, can be chosen as a deterministic function of
({V}(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}),\,\left(\frac{9}{8n}\right)^{1/4}\cdot d_{\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}})
,
n\geq 2
, only; that is in a way which does not depend on the measures
(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}})_{n}
.
One (artificial) way to enforce this is to apply Proposition
3.4
to the convergence

\displaystyle\left({V}(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}),\,\left(\frac{9}{8n}\right)^{1/4}\cdot d_{\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}},\,0\right)\xrightarrow[n\rightarrow\infty]{(\mathrm{d}),\,\mathrm{GHP}}(S,d,0).

This allows to see the measures
(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}})_{n}
as random measures on the compact space
(Z,\delta)
, such that the collection
(\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}}(v_{j}),1\leq j\leq N_{n})
is exchangeable
22
22

22

We recall that we are reasoning conditionally on
(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n},n\geq 2)
and
(\sup_{x}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}}(x),n\geq 2)
.

and satisfies
\sup_{x}\frac{1}{n+2}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}}(x)\to 0
almost surely.

In particular, by Proposition
6.1
, if we set
D_{n}=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{N_{n}}\}
, then

\displaystyle\updelta_{\mathrm{P}}\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{n+2}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}},\tfrac{|\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}}|}{n+2}\cdot\mathrm{Unif}_{D_{n}}\Bigr)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0.

Now by construction, the mass
|\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}}|
counts those vertices among the
n+2
ones of
Q^{\prime}_{n}
(defined in Lemma
2.1
) which are not incident to its unique largest component.
Hence
|\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}}|=(n+2)-(|\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}|+2)
, which is asymptotically equivalent to
8n/9
by the bound on
K_{n}
in Lemma
2.1
.
In particular, we can turn the last display into

(6.8)

\displaystyle\updelta_{\mathrm{P}}\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{n+2}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\mathfrak{D}_{n}},\tfrac{8}{9}\cdot\mathrm{Unif}_{D_{n}}\Bigr)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0.

Now
\mathrm{Unif}_{D_{n}}
is the uniform measure on the set
\{v_{1},\dots,v_{N_{n}}\}
which contains one vertex incident to each face.
That is, it projects the uniform measure on the face-set of
\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}
to vertices incident to each face.
Since the faces have diameter
O(n^{-1/4})
under
\left(\frac{9}{8n}\right)^{1/4}\cdot d_{\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}}
, it is easily seen that
\mathrm{Unif}_{D_{n}}
is an approximation of the measure which instead spreads the uniform measure on the face-set of
\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}
evenly on the vertices incident to each face, namely the degree-biased measure
\mu^{\mathrm{deg}}_{n}
on
V(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n})
which gives mass
\deg(v)/2|E(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n})|
to a vertex
v
.
Using a deterministic comparison, due to Addario-Berry and Wen
[
6
, Lemma 5.1]
, between the degree-biased measure and the uniform measure on vertices of any quadrangulation, we therefore conclude that
\mu^{\mathrm{deg}}_{n}
is asymptotically close in the Prokhorov metric to the uniform measure on
V(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n})
, which is itself close to
\frac{9}{n}\mu_{\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}}
since
V(\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n})\sim n/9
in probability.
The conclusion of this whole discussion is that:

(6.9)

\displaystyle\updelta_{\mathrm{P}}\Bigl(\tfrac{8}{9}\cdot\mathrm{Unif}_{D_{n}},\frac{8}{n+2}\mu_{\mathfrak{Q}^{{\mathrm{irr}}}_{n}}\cdot\Bigr)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0.

Wrapping up, (
6.7
), (
6.8
), and (
6.9
) together yield the convergence
